Theon · THEON-NL · the new Memo conveyor's card of 31 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Theon

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 45,534 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

A842nd of 30

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces give the Consumer a high-confidence revenue path to 2028 and a low-confidence margin and multiple path. Demand is structural and contracted: 1.46 EURbn of backlog, 902 EURm of options, 500 EURm scheduled for FY2027, and policy floors to 2035. So the top line should be modelled as supply- and schedule-constrained.

Three external forces set the error bars:

  1. Tube availability. One supplier, a Chinese raw material, and OCCAR absorbing most of Theon's contracted Exosens volume in 2027 to 2029. It caps NVG growth at roughly tube-volume-plus-price and makes non-NVG growth the swing factor for consensus.
  2. Agency procurement pricing. OCCAR is "priced competitively", and its bulk delivery is still ahead.
  3. The cost of money and the sector's multiple. Both act on a company that has chosen to lever up at the top of the order cycle.

Fundamentally the external change picture is a strong A-grade tailwind. For the investor it is a C, because the forces that reach the share price (rates, credit, sector de-rating, ceasefire headlines) are adverse in the current regime.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0255C-0532C-0733C-0945C-3555C-3940
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-4411th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitive intensity: how much is it changing, and in which direction?

Judgement: intensity is stable to easing in the core and rising at the edges the strategy is moving into. Because the mix is shifting toward those edges, the group-level direction is a gentle tightening.

  • Core (European man-portable night vision):
  • Theon holds 50% to 60%; Safran and Thales 12% to 13% each, and both are reported to "avoid markets where Theon is strong".
  • The US primes are ITAR-blocked from exporting above about 1,800 to 2,000 Figure of Merit, and are absorbed by the 1.27 USDbn BiNOD programme.
  • Edges, all tightening in the last six to twelve months:
  • Exosens has moved downstream: it bought NVLS in July 2025, won 17,000 Spanish monoculars, and Photonis Defense assembles BiNOD units in the US as a prime.
  • Hensoldt's Optronics orders were 759 EURm in Q1 2026, with Oberkochen capacity and a margin rising to 9.7% to 12.2%.
  • Thales won a German land-forces vision framework of several hundred units a month.
  • NextVision is scaling 5,000 gimbals a month.
  • Digital entrants are arriving: Anduril EagleEye, and Sage Technologies (October 2026).
  • Direction for the model: the core NVG gross margin is protected by structure. The platform, gimbal and ISR lines will be priced into contested markets; Deutsche Bank expects initial platform margins to be lower, and Kappa runs at about 20% EBIT. The diversification therefore buys growth with a lower-margin, more competitive revenue line.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F2230th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a net headwind, and an intensifying one. It bites through availability, which caps volume, more than through price, which is mostly passed through. It is the single external force that could stop the backlog converting.

  • Concentration:
  • Tubes are 60% to 65% of goggle manufacturing cost.
  • Exosens supplied about 75% of Theon's tube spend, and Theon is 40% of Exosens' revenue.
  • Exosens holds about 80% of the non-US market.
  • Tube lead times are 18 months.
  • Price:
  • Exosens has raised tube prices by over 5% a year.
  • Bernstein models Exosens' amplification selling price rising from 2,531 EUR (2025) to 2,898 EUR (2029), about 3.4% a year.
  • Theon claims back-to-back pass-through, but its fixed-price frameworks (OCCAR) lack indexation, so any unpriced rise is absorbed.
  • Availability:
  • Gallium is about 95% refined in China; indium gallium arsenide is squeezed by AI demand; skilled optical technicians are scarce in Greece and Germany.
  • Exosens' own OCCAR order absorbs about 43% of its tube output through 2029.
  • Mitigants, all improving over the last six months:
  • The agreement runs to end-2030 with priority.
  • Harder Digital was raised to 65.22% (24 July 2026), with capacity at 15,000 tubes rising to 20,000 in 2027, Figure of Merit above 2,000, and internal absorption of about 66%.
  • The designs are tube-agnostic.
  • Modelling implication, a rough cross-check of my own, not a disclosed figure:
  • About 80,000 Exosens tubes a year plus about 13,000 Harder tubes for internal use, at roughly 5,000 to 6,000 EUR of NVG revenue per tube, gives an NVG revenue ceiling of the order of 500 to 600 EURm.
  • It rises only with tube price and any extra Exosens allocation.
  • NVG revenue growth after 2026 should therefore be modelled at tube-volume-plus-price, high single digit to low teens; Deutsche Bank caps NVG growth at about 15%. The rest of consensus growth must come from non-NVG.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3227th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Macro: how much is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: macro bites hardest at the company level, through the cost and availability of debt and through the equity discount rate on a small, geared, founder-controlled stock. It bites next at the industry level, through the de-rating and the rotation, and least at the sector level, where electronics have held up.

  • Company:
  • The US ten-year yield is above 5%; euro investment-grade and high-yield yields are up 30 and 90 basis points since September; the house tilt is Fragility Double Underweight.
  • Theon is moving to about 3x leverage on floating-rate facilities: the revolving facility at Euribor plus 1.5% (a 100 basis point move costs about 0.9 EURm on the old base) and a 325 EURm term loan to 2031.
  • Deutsche Bank has already cut EPS 5% for interest.
  • Industry:
  • Land-defence EV/EBIT fell from 21.6x to 17.2x (January to September 2026), and the sector premium to the STOXX 600 fell from 60% to 12%. The house tilt on the industry is Underweight.
  • Sector:
  • The non-primes are Neutral; MilDef, Hensoldt, Exail and Exosens held up.
  • Demand is insulated (euro-area GDP 0.8% in 2026, 1.2% in 2027, with defence spending supporting it). FX is small (a 10% USD move is 1.57 EURm of FY2025 profit).
  • For the model: stress the interest line, a +100 basis point all-in cost, rather than revenue for macro.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three, and the net is negative for the share price while neutral for the operations.

  1. The sector's valuation regime. Exit multiples are normalising toward about 13x EV/EBIT, and estimates are holding while multiples fall. Theon is already near that level (my estimate: about 13x to 14x FY2027 EV/EBIT after adding HGH's cash cost and deducting the Exosens stake), so further sector compression has less room to hurt Theon than its peers, which trade at 20x to 28x P/E.
  2. The Exosens share price as an external input to Theon's accounts. The 9.8% stake (cost 268.7 EURm, plus 37.2 EURm of fair-value gains by June 2026) is about 11% to 13% of Theon's market capitalisation. It is marked through reported EPS (0.47 EUR of H1 2026's 0.96 EUR). It is also the main asset-sale route for deleveraging, so Exosens' multiple (21.2x, 56th percentile) now matters to Theon's balance sheet.
  3. Geopolitical headline risk in both directions. Iran and Middle-East escalation since March 2026 support demand (Asia rose to 85 EURm in FY2025 from 24 EURm). Ceasefire and summit headlines (19 October 2026) hit sentiment.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F4024th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading New entrants: how much are they entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: the adjacent entrant is the material threat; standard entrants are negligible; orthogonal entrants are a medium-term threat to the high end.

  • Adjacent (medium to high in Europe, high in specialist niches): Exosens/NVLS and Photonis Defense. The supplier of 60% to 65% of Theon's cost now sells finished goggles and has a 5G-tube head start.
  • Standard (low): niche European and Asian assemblers, held back by two-to-eight-year qualification and tube scarcity.
  • Orthogonal (low to 2028, medium after): Anduril EagleEye, Sage digital goggles, NextVision gimbals, and drone start-ups competing for the same budgets.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F2529th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Suppliers: how are they behaving, and are they a support to the company or a source of risk?

Judgement: both at once, and the balance is risk. Exosens behaves as a supportive partner bound by mutual dependency, but its capacity, pricing and downstream ambitions are outside Theon's control, and Harder is a hedge only for non-NATO work.

  • Support:
  • Well over 400,000 tubes over 2026–2030 with priority production, about 52% to 55% of Exosens' tube volume.
  • Faster delivery than rivals.
  • Back-to-back pricing; relationship pricing between the chief executives.
  • Exosens raised FY2026 guidance on a faster capacity ramp (17 September 2026).
  • Risk:
  • Price rises of over 5% a year historically.
  • No disclosed indexation caps in the Theon agreement.
  • Exosens' OCCAR share (200,000 tubes over 2027–2029) concentrates Theon's supply on one programme.
  • Exosens' own monitoring threshold flags Theon buying fewer than 70,000 tubes a year.
  • Gallium.
  • Harder:
  • NATO agencies avoid its tubes because of historic Serbia and Russia links; experts call them "Tier-2".
  • It is useful for price discipline and non-NATO bids.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0182C-0360C-0474C-0876C-3274C-3770C-4372
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

A881st of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading End-market demand: how much is it changing, structurally or cyclically, and with what visibility?

Judgement: demand is a structural, policy-backed swing, and it is contracted to 2029. In the forecast window the binding variable is how fast budgets convert into contracts and deliveries, not whether demand exists. The open question is the shape after 2028–29, and the market is now pricing that exit.

  • Last six months against the prior 12 to 18:
  • H1 2026 order intake rose 38.5% to 232.5 EURm, and Q2 was the strongest non-Q4 quarter in the group's history.
  • The soft backlog rose to 1.46 EURbn plus 902 EURm of options. At end-2023 it was 540 EURm, and at end-2024 654 EURm.
  • A further c. 50 EURm of orders and pre-selection for "several higher-value contracts" were announced on 30 September 2026.
  • Across Morgan Stanley's coverage, European defence orders rose 25% to over 85 EURbn and the aggregate backlog reached 438 EURbn.
  • Policy is a floor: NATO 3.5% of GDP core by 2035, Germany 180 EURbn by 2030, SAFE 150 EURbn.
  • The 1:1 soldier-equipping shift and Europe's 30% NVG penetration against nearly 100% in the US are a volume multiplier.
  • The visibility counts as cyclical with two-plus years. About 80% of initial annual guidance is covered at the start of each year, and 500 EURm of FY2027 is scheduled.
  • The caveats are about pace, not existence:
  • German annualised spending runs at 84 EURbn against budget targets, and purchases above 25 EURm wait for the Bundestag budget committee.
  • Italy is trimming its planned increase.
  • Frame contracts "often materialise at much lower actual spend".
  • Q1 2026 intake fell 40.6% on phasing.
  • Former Theon managers date an analog-NVG demand peak at 2027 to 2029.

For the model: treat 2026 to 2028 revenue as supply- and schedule-driven, not demand-driven. Make option exercise (902 EURm) and OCCAR phasing the explicit timing variables. Stress 2029 and beyond with a replacement-demand assumption.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B605th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Disruption: how much is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: the technology disruption to the analog core is being disproved for the forecast horizon. The real disruption is a shift of budgets and doctrine toward drones, counter-drone systems and fused, networked systems. That shift acts on Theon in both directions, and on balance Theon has bought itself onto the right side of it.

  • Disproved near term:
  • Digital night vision costs 5 to 8 times as much as tube goggles, with latency, battery and weight penalties.
  • Analog is expected to dominate for three to five years, perhaps a decade (experts; Exosens management).
  • Strengthening:
  • Fused goggles: Theon's ORION won a Western European NATO order for a fully interconnected soldier system.
  • Augmented-reality overlays (ARMED Next prototype before end-2026), AI targeting (Twin Prime, HGH's Cyclope), drone gimbals (Merio, Safran 51% joint venture) and counter-drone ISR (HGH, about 40 EURm of revenue at over 40% EBITDA margin).
  • Where it hurts:
  • Middle-East and Asian budgets diverting to counter-drone systems could slow infantry NVG replenishment.
  • Intellectual property is moving to software, where Theon is a buyer, not an originator: R&D is 2.8% of revenue.
  • For the model: no displacement haircut to NVG volumes before 2029. The disruption appears as faster non-NVG mix (24% of Q2 2026 revenue against 3% a year earlier) and as higher acquired R&D and opex in the cost base.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

A881st of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Political or regulatory change: how much is under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: the regulatory current is strongly in Theon's favour and has hardened in the last six months. The political risk is to pace and to multiples, not to the rules.

  • Tailwinds:
  • SAFE requires at least 65% of component cost from the EU, the EEA or Ukraine.
  • EDIP targets 50% European sourcing by 2030; design authority must reside in the EU/EEA.
  • Omnibus V (September 2026) lengthens frameworks from seven to ten years, which suits OCCAR-type multi-year awards.
  • ITAR keeps US primes out.
  • Greek Development Law 4887/2022 gives a 20% tax exemption on the 10 EURm Athens expansion.
  • SAFE directly funded the 42 EURm Kappa vehicle-awareness order.
  • Headwinds:
  • Multi-jurisdiction antitrust and foreign-investment review ended the Exosens board-seat plan.
  • Dual-use export authorisations are the primary operational tail risk (Deutsche Bank), and the EU list was updated in September 2026.
  • German approvals above 25 EURm are a timing gate (Bundestag vote 27 November 2026).
  • French fiscal stress and the 2027 election.
  • US pressure through FMS and sanction relief on Russia (19 October 2026) moved sector sentiment.
  • For the model: a structural share protection in Europe and a lower effective tax rate. Phase revenue around approvals and export licences rather than budgets.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

A901st of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Industry or sector driver: how much is it accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: strongly, and more for Theon than for most of the sector, because its product sits at the intersection of four accelerating drivers.

  • The four drivers: soldier modernisation on a 1:1 basis, agency/framework procurement (OCCAR), sovereign ITAR-free content rules, and the sector's order boom.
  • Defence optronics grows at about twice the rate of total defence spending (a 12% to 13% CAGR).
  • OCCAR-type agency buying is expected to reach 40% of European procurement by 2030. It favours the largest qualified supplier, and Theon took the largest NVG award in NATO history.
  • The limit: the driver is accelerating demand faster than the supply chain can deliver it. The acceleration shows as a longer backlog, not as faster revenue beyond the tube cap.
  • The cost: agency buying shifts pricing power to the buyer on the largest contracts.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A821st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Customer demand: how is it behaving, how much does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: strongly supportive and contracted; fragile only in timing and concentration.

  • Behaviour:
  • Customers are sovereign, moving to multi-year frameworks, standardised off-the-shelf buying and 1:1 equipping.
  • Q4 budget-clearance spending suits NVGs.
  • About 300,000 systems are in service, which generates replacement and upgrade demand (thermal clip-ons, the 70 EURm German IdZ order, the 100 EURm German clip-on framework).
  • Fragility:
  • One customer is 38% of revenue.
  • The 902 EURm of options depend on ministries' call-off timing.
  • Procurement cycles compressed to 3 to 12 months during the Ukraine war would revert to 30-plus months after a ceasefire (a Rheinmetall programme manager).
  • For the model: probability-weight option exercise. Put the downside scenario's timing slip in 2027 to 2028, not 2026.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

A851st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: strongly helpful, with fragility in political appetite and process speed rather than in rules.

  • Helpful: NATO's floor, SAFE and EDIP content rules, Omnibus V, the Greek tax incentive, and ITAR shielding.
  • Hurtful: antitrust (it ended the Exosens plan), export authorisations, the Bundestag approval cadence, French fiscal stress and the election, and Italy's cut.
  • Fragility: political appetite for 3.5% of GDP would weaken after a ceasefire, though Morgan Stanley argues the cycle is now independent of Ukraine. The UK Budget of 28 October 2026 funded its defence investment plan but deferred the 3% decision.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

B+825th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Addressable market: how large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what growth does it allow over the mid term?

Judgement: large enough to allow above-15% organic growth to 2029. The binding limit on the core is the tube supply, not market size, and the extra headroom is in the adjacencies.

  • Core size: tactical optronics is 2.7 USDbn (2022) rising to 4.6 USDbn by 2027 (a 13% CAGR). European NVG penetration is 30% against nearly 100% in the US. The move from monocular to binocular doubles tubes per soldier.
  • Wider market: management cites about 8 EURbn after platforms, ISR, gimbals and counter-drone.
  • Theon's FY2026 revenue of about 600 EURm is under 10% of that wider market and about half of European man-portable.
  • The mid-term range of 12% to 20% a year is supported; DB caps NVG growth at about 15%.

Overall external picture

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F4525th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading The company's own revenue and demand cycle: where is it, and how much is it turning?

Judgement: the order cycle peaked in FY2025 and revenue growth peaks in FY2026; from here Theon is in the conversion phase of its cycle.

  • Order cycle: FY2025 intake was a record 1.3 EURbn, about a 3.0x book-to-bill. In 2026 the book-to-bill is about 1.0x (H1).
  • Revenue: growth re-accelerated from +25.8% (FY2025) to +35.4% (H1 2026; 24.7% organic, Kappa 11 points). Guidance implies H2 2026 at about 351 EURm, +35% on H2 2025.
  • Consensus decelerates from here: +23.8% (FY2027, including HGH and Merio), +17.0% (FY2028), +13.6% (FY2029). That is consistent with a conversion phase and with the tube cap.
  • The stock now trades like the sector, on delivery, margin and cash. The sector has moved "from an order-driven phase to an execution-driven phase" (Bernstein, 29 September 2026), and the market "no longer pays more for a share because the company wins an order".
  • The next six months are the Q4 order season:
  • The 902 EURm of options are "expected to be exercised shortly".
  • The pre-selected contracts would convert soft visibility into firm backlog.
  • A book-to-bill below 1.0x at FY2026 would be the first sign that the backlog is being consumed faster than it is replenished.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

A782nd of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the fundamental external picture is among the strongest in the universe, while the external forces acting on the share price and the cost of capital are adverse. For the model, the external forces support revenue and gross margin, threaten volume timing through supply, and tax the below-EBIT lines and the multiple.

Each thematic driver in turn:

  • European rearmament budgets: a strong tailwind, structural to 2035. The headwind is the pace of conversion.
  • Soldier modernisation and 1:1 equipping: a strong tailwind, and Theon's core.
  • Sovereign European supply (Buy European, ITAR-free): a strong tailwind, and intensifying.
  • Drones, counter-drone and digital fusion: a mixed driver. It diverts some budgets, and it is Theon's diversification route.
  • Critical-input scarcity (tubes, gallium, labour): a headwind, and intensifying.
  • Sector valuation and positioning normalisation: a headwind to the multiple.
  • Rates and credit: a headwind to EPS and to the multiple for a geared small cap.

By level:

  • Value chain: demand is a strong tailwind and the supplier side is a headwind.
  • Industry: order books are a tailwind and the de-rating is a headwind.
  • Sector: electronics and optronics are favoured over land and ammunition, but Theon is being priced on land-defence multiples because of its leverage.

Net: a tailwind on operations and a headwind on valuation and financing.

2

Internal change forces/risks

C+678th of 30

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

This is a founder-led corporate transformation that is real, already in the revenue mix, and funded by leverage at the top of the order cycle.

  • The positive forces (guidance raised twice, new products selling, coherent European acquisitions, a stronger bench) support revenue and EBITDA upgrades.
  • The negative forces share one root: debt-funded, fast, founder-directed expansion with thin governance. The negatives are leverage near covenant, capital-allocation volatility, related-party loans, disclosure lapses and rising concentration.
  • So in a stress year they correlate. The chain is a ceasefire delaying options, EBITDA undershooting, the covenant binding, and an equity raise.
  • For Set 2:
  • Model the internal change as adding acquired revenue and margin mix from 2027.
  • Explicitly model the below-EBIT cost of the change.
  • Treat the January 2027 board renewal, the HGH closing and the H1 2027 leverage print as the three internal checkpoints.

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F3222nd of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: a cluster of individually tolerable negatives. They share one root, which is fast, debt-funded, founder-directed expansion, and so they would compound together in a downturn.

  • Leverage moving to 3.0x to 3.1x against a 3.5x covenant: about 0.4x to 0.5x of headroom, roughly a 15% EBITDA cushion at peak, by my arithmetic.
  • Capital-allocation volatility: the Exosens board-seat plan was abandoned within a year. That leaves 268.7 EURm in a supplier with no control and no income line, and its marks swing reported EPS.
  • Related-party loans of 82.66 EURm, about 22% of total assets, interest-free and with no repayment date: a KPMG key audit matter.
  • Customer concentration rising: one European customer was 38% of FY2025 revenue, against 30% in FY2024. In my reading OCCAR keeps it near that level through 2028.
  • Working capital at 41% of revenue, partly structural: quick payment to suppliers and tube stockpiling to keep priority.
  • Disclosure lapses: the Q1 2026 cash-flow error, the joint-venture reclassification uplift, and the IFRS 15 restatement.
  • Thin disclosure: no segment margins and no interest guidance.
  • Dilution precedent: the December 2025 rights issue at 17.40 EUR.

Group judgement

Risks

D4015th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The core growth engine carries no deceleration risk inside 18 months. The risks are of two shapes.

  • Sudden supply or customer shocks. Each is hedged and low-probability.
  • A slower-burning combination. A ceasefire delays OCCAR tranches and options in the same year that HGH's 290 EURm goes out. LTM EBITDA then undershoots against a roughly 15% covenant cushion, and the fix is a discounted equity raise.

Leverage is what converts a timing problem into a shareholder loss.

  • Perception risk is high for a small, controlled, geared stock in a risk-off tape.
  • The trough downside is about 20% to 28%, and about 35% to 40% in stress.

For Set 3:

  • The predictability of FY2026–FY2027 revenue is high.
  • The predictability of EPS and of net debt is medium-to-low.
  • The single most diagnostic data points are the FY2026 book-to-bill and option exercise, the year-end 2026 and H1 2027 leverage, and the H2 2026 and H1 2027 gross margin as OCCAR volumes ramp.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C559th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: concentrated. Three drivers explain most of the difference between the next four years and the last six, and most of the variance of FY2027: the timing of OCCAR and German call-offs against a fixed tube supply; the working-capital ratio; and the HGH contribution against its debt cost. A fourth, Exosens' share price, moves reported earnings but nothing real. Concentration makes the case easy to monitor and hard to diversify: the three drivers are correlated through leverage, so one slip moves every line at once.

The differences between the forecast and the history, and the driver behind each:

Difference History (FY2020 to FY2025) Forecast (FY2026 to FY2030) Driver that creates the difference
Growth halves, then halves again revenue +52% a year +36%, +24%, +13%, +11%, +9% the tube cap (Exosens 80,000 to 90,000 plus Harder 13,000 a year) and the OCCAR schedule replace demand pull; non-night-vision lines and acquisitions supply the rest
The margin stops falling FactSet EBIT margin 31.6% to 23.4% company-basis flat at 26.2% to 26.5% OCCAR dilution (-0.6 to -0.8 points on night vision) against HGH and tube-free mix (+0.6 to +0.8); localisation and wages absorb the efficiencies
Share issuance stops, interest starts share count +26% FY2023 to FY2026; interest 1 to 10 EURm shares flat; interest 18 to 27 EURm the 325 EURm term loan funding HGH, with no Exosens dividend to offset
Cash flow turns from erratic to rising free cash flow 21, -42, 14 EURm 32, 63, 94, 120, 138 EURm growth slowing (absorption falls from 48 to 27 EURm a year) more than the ratio falling (42.5% to 38.5%)
Return on capital halves ROCE 31% (FY2024) about 14% to 15% about 560 EURm of acquisition goodwill, intangibles and the non-earning Exosens stake
Mix night vision 93% non-night-vision 21% to 41% Kappa, HGH, Merio, thermal clip-ons; night vision flat from FY2028

How concentrated, by line and by year (my arithmetic on the base case):

  • FY2027 revenue growth of 147 EURm splits into night vision +52 (tube volume plus 1.5% price), acquired +62 (HGH 42, Merio 15, the Safran joint venture 5) and non-night-vision organic +33. Two drivers, OCCAR phasing on fixed tube supply and the HGH close, are 77% of it. The OWN-1 report's residual of about 23,000 tubes a year for non-OCCAR customers in 2027 means that if OCCAR runs to schedule, the rest of night vision is nearly fixed; if OCCAR slips, tubes can be redirected to bilateral and export orders at better margins, which is the one natural hedge in the system, limited by the 18-month lead time on new orders.
  • FY2027 adjusted EPS growth of 0.28 EUR (1.35 to 1.63): EBIT +39.6 EURm adds 0.38 after tax; interest -9 EURm takes 0.09; minorities take 0.01. The EBIT part is diffuse (night vision, mix, HGH); the interest part is one driver (net debt times rate).
  • End-2027 leverage of about 2.48x: set by two numbers, working capital (each point of revenue is 7.5 EURm, or 0.035x) and EBITDA (each 10 EURm is 0.1x). The 290 EURm HGH payment is known.
  • From FY2028 the drivers diffuse: revenue growth of 101 EURm splits night vision +35, non-night-vision +40, acquired +26; and the growth-slows-so-cash-rises mechanism (P-05) needs no single decision.

The sensitivities that rank the drivers (FY2027, base case):

  1. An OCCAR or German call-off slip of one quarter (about 30 EURm of revenue, 10% of OCCAR's year): revenue -4%, EBIT -5% to -6% (the fixed block is committed), adjusted EPS -7%, leverage +0.15x. Base rate about one in three for comparable German programmes since 2023. This is the largest and the most likely.
  2. Working capital one point worse (41% to 42%): 7.5 EURm of cash, 0.035x of leverage, 0.3 EURm of interest. Small per point, but the record is of three points of miss against consensus.
  3. HGH closing one quarter later: -12 EURm of revenue and -4.5 EURm of EBIT, offset by about 3 EURm of interest saved; EPS about -0.01 EUR. Timing is nearly neutral for EPS and positive for the leverage print at the June 2027 test (one fewer quarter of debt). It is the one driver where a delay helps the balance sheet.
  4. 100 basis points on Euribor: about 6 EURm, or -0.06 EUR (-4%) of EPS, with swap coverage undisclosed.
  5. Exosens' share price ±10%: ±28 EURm of reported pre-tax profit, ±0.36 EUR of reported EPS, zero cash, zero covenant effect (the stake is not netted) but a 2.8% move in the value of the deleveraging option.
  6. Tax rate ±1 point: ±0.02 EUR.
  7. A 5% revenue beat from non-night-vision: EBIT +6%, EPS +7%; night vision cannot supply a beat beyond its tubes. The upside drivers are fewer than the downside drivers, which is the asymmetry the second set found.

How the drivers correlate, which is what makes concentration dangerous: a ceasefire or a Bundestag delay slows call-offs (driver 1), which leaves inventory bought for those deliveries on the balance sheet (driver 2), which raises net debt in the same half-year that HGH's cash goes out (driver 3), while the sector sell-off that accompanies a ceasefire also marks the Exosens stake down (driver 5), weakening the one lever that would relieve the covenant. The May to June 2026 episode showed the sector leg (peers -30%, Theon -4.8%) without the operating leg, because deliveries were not yet OCCAR-weighted. From Q1 2027 both legs would move together.

What the concentration implies for the Consumer: a short monitoring list (Bundestag approvals and Hensoldt's order intake as the proxy for German call-offs; Exosens' quarterly tube volumes; Theon's quarterly working-capital ratio and joint-venture receivables; the HGH closing date) covers most of the variance, which is favourable for trackability. But the forecast cannot be de-risked by diversification inside the company: there is no second large customer, no second tube supplier for NATO work, and no second deleveraging lever beyond the stake. The precedent for suppliers dependent on one framework and one supplier (Hensoldt on F126, L3Harris on sole-source subcontractors, Exosens itself at 40% Theon) is of wider outcome dispersion than diversified peers show.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4716th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Pricing: how much is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: there are no demonstrated real price rises. Theon's strategy is to price 15% to 20% below Western European peers and to pass tube inflation through. The recent gross-margin gain is mix, not price. Pricing power is latent: present in the scarcity market, deliberately not used.

  • For:
  • Industry NVG device prices rose 2.5x to 3.0x since 2022.
  • ING reports "zero pricing pressure in core NVG".
  • The gross margin rose to 35.1% (H1 2026) from 32.7%.
  • ING says newer frameworks carry "efficient raw material cost pass-throughs".
  • Against:
  • The industry price rise followed tube costs.
  • The gross margin fell from 40.7% (FY2021) to 31.0% (FY2024) as contracts grew larger and volume discounts deepened.
  • OCCAR is "priced competitively", and DB cut EBIT 3% on it.
  • The CFO acknowledged that large contracts need steeper discounts.
  • Management talks about cost advantage, not price.
  • For the model: price contributes roughly the pass-through of tube inflation, about 3% to 5% a year on the NVG line, at constant gross-margin percentage. It does not contribute real margin. Mix drives the margin.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B707th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strong and broad, well-resourced financially and thinly resourced organisationally. Their cost lands below EBIT, so they improve revenue and EBITDA faster than earnings per share until 2028.

  • Breadth: six of twelve internal forces are at B or better: strategy, guidance, geographies, products, M&A and balance-sheet use.
  • Evidence of translation: non-NVG at 24% of Q2 2026 revenue; Kappa adding 11 points of growth; guidance raised twice.
  • Weak spots: shareholder returns run backwards, the perimeter is widening, pricing is not used, and governance has not moved.
  • Resourcing: debt and equity are ample. Management bandwidth and board oversight are not, given four integrations in four countries.
  • The shape the Consumer should model:
  • 2026–2027: EBITDA growth of about 30% a year, EPS growth of about 20%. Interest rises from 9.5 EURm (FY2025) toward about 20 to 25 EURm, and the share count is up about 12%.
  • 2028 onward: the leak narrows as debt falls.

The Investment Analyst's heading Staircase of novel positive developments: which new, dated things can the market look forward to in the next 6 months, months 6 to 18, and months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well founded?

Judgement: a dense, dated, company-delivered staircase through 2027. Its novelty is moderate, because each step is known and discussed, and the step that would most change the narrative, visible deleveraging, sits at months 6 to 12. The near steps are about orders, which the market no longer pays for; the steps that would re-rate the stock are about cash and leverage.

Months 18 to 36:

  • Leverage at about 2.0x by FY2028.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

A762nd of 30

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

A762nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The structure Theon sits in is about as supportive as defence offers. It is a concentrated, margin-first integrator oligopoly with Theon at number one, protected by qualification lock-in, ITAR and Buy-European rules, and consolidating toward it through agency procurement.

The value chain has two pressure points, and both act on margin rather than on volume share:

  • The buyer, through agency procurement: priced on volume and rising toward 40% of European buying.
  • The supplier: a co-dependent monopolist that is also a new downstream competitor and Theon's investee.

For the model:

  • European NVG share stays stable.
  • NVG pricing tracks tube cost.
  • The gross-margin path is mix-driven: non-NVG and HGH up, OCCAR down.
  • Customer concentration should be treated as persistent at about 35% to 40% while OCCAR is delivered.
  • The precedent that matters most is that the sector pays for delivery and cash after an order boom, and that a full-order-book fall reverses only when the company's own cash arrives.

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4228th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Route to market and intermediaries: how much do they help or hinder the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: they help with access but cost margin and add dependency. The biggest route, the Hensoldt joint venture for OCCAR, is held by a company that is also a competitor in platforms.

  • OCCAR invoicing flows through Hensoldt–Theon GbR: 130.67 EURm of 2025 sales, with 34.95 EURm of receivables at year-end. A former manager says OCCAR flows there "because Hensoldt is German".
  • Other routes: JK Defence (German police and after-sales), Sunrock (French law enforcement, 8,500 ARGUS units), and Elbit Systems of America (NYX kits under a 500 USDm ID/IQ, as a lower-margin subcontractor).
  • Middle-East offsets force local brands (Thaqib).
  • Rheinmetall is the prime for PHYLAX.
  • Fragilities:
  • The working capital tied up in joint-venture receivables.
  • Hensoldt's growing Optronics ambitions; it won the 400 EURm Boxer and Leopard 2 optronics award from KNDS.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C+558th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Disruption forces in the value chain: on whom are they acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: disruption is acting on every actor around Theon. The net leaves the core robust and the edges and financing fragile.

  • Demand: budgets shift toward drones, counter-drone, electronic warfare and software. Theon partly captures this through Merio, HGH and Safran.
  • Competition: the supplier is turning competitor (Exosens/NVLS), and primes are expanding optronics.
  • Regulators and political actors: Buy-European rules hardcode Theon's eligibility, while antitrust blocks its vertical integration.
  • Suppliers:
  • AI demand competes for indium gallium arsenide, and China controls gallium.
  • Exosens' own capacity is committed: OCCAR takes 43% of its output, Sturbridge starts in early 2027, and the 5G ramp is under way.
  • Capital markets: credit tightening and sector de-rating act on the cost of Theon's chosen leverage.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A882nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Concentration: how concentrated is the market, and how steep is the share distribution?

Judgement: highly concentrated and steep at both the integrator and the component layer, which favours a margin-first equilibrium.

  • Integrator layer: the top three hold about 75% to 85% of European man-portable night vision, well above 30% for the top five. The niche players (NightVision Lasers Spain, OIP, Senop, PCO; EOS and Hamamatsu in Asia) are in single digits, so the curve is far steeper than "five times numbers six to ten".
  • Component layer: more concentrated still. Exosens has about 80% of the non-US tube market and is the only at-scale ITAR-free supplier; Harder is sub-scale and Tier-2.
  • US market: a closed L3Harris–Elbit duopoly, with Photonis Defense now entering.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

A852nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Share stability: how stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: shares have moved in one direction, toward Theon, and new entrants have taken negligible share. The one meaningful entrant is the upstream monopolist moving downstream.

  • Theon's rise: from a regional supplier to 50% to 60% of Europe, at revenue growth of about 40% to 60% a year from 2020 to 2024.
  • Customer retention: Sweden has been a customer since 1997, with over 25,000 units.
  • Entrants: qualification cycles of two to eight years keep standard entrants out. Exosens/NVLS took the 17,000-monocular Spanish order, and ACTinBlack bought 7,000 5G tubes direct for special forces. These are small wins, but they establish a direct sovereign-customer channel for the supplier.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

A881st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Consolidation: how much does share consolidate over time toward the largest and toward the most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: yes on both counts, and Theon is both the largest and the most advantaged in its core. Agency procurement accelerates the consolidation.

  • OCCAR-type joint buying turns many national tenders into one large award. That favours the qualified supplier with cost, scale and tube priority, and the 1 EURbn award is the proof.
  • Mechanisms: installed base, qualification lock-in, about 52% of Exosens' tube volume to 2030, and a 15% to 20% price advantage.
  • The flip side: the same consolidation moves bargaining power to the buyer, with discounts on 100,000-unit awards. It also concentrates Theon's revenue on fewer, larger customers.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

B+785th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitor attitude: do competitors put margin and return first or volume first, are they benign, and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: benign and margin-first among the primes. Prices fall with volume only in agency tenders. The disciplining force on industry price is the buyer and the supplier, not rivalry.

  • Safran and Thales prioritise high-end integrated systems with disciplined pricing.
  • Export contracts earn about 30% margins against 20% domestic.
  • The US primes are capacity-constrained and domestically absorbed.
  • Exosens is restrained from squeezing Theon by mutual dependency: Theon is 40% of its revenue.
  • Prices fall with volume on agency awards (the 100,000-unit OCCAR), not on bilateral national awards. A stronger agency share means a lower average price per unit, even without rivalry.

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Stability of that attitude: how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years, and would competitors launch a "shark attack" on pricing?

Judgement: stable in the core and more aggressive at the edges. The plausible "sharks" are not the incumbent primes.

  • Getting more aggressive:
  • Hensoldt's Optronics capacity.
  • The Thales German framework "in the mid-four-figure price range".
  • NextVision's gimbal scale.
  • Venture-funded digital entrants (Anduril, Sage).
  • Plausible sharks:
  • Exosens, which could prioritise its own devices or "higher-margin 5G tubes for competitors".
  • Subsidised Middle-East sovereign players built on offset-transferred IP (EDGE and GAMI partners).
  • Digital start-ups pricing to win scale once costs fall.
  • None of these threatens core NVG pricing inside 18 months. Platform pricing is already competitive by Deutsche Bank's account.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

A763rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: very supportive in the core, a benign concentrated oligopoly with Theon at number one, and fragile in three specific places.

  1. Buyer power through agency procurement, rising to 40% of European buying by 2030.
  2. The upstream monopolist integrating downstream into Theon's market while also being Theon's supplier and investee.
  3. Customer concentration, with 38% from one customer through one German joint venture.

None is likely to break inside the 18-month window; all three cap the margin upside in the 18-month to four-year window.

The Investment Analyst's heading Substitutes: how much of a threat are other ways for customers to solve the same problems, and how is that changing?

Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

B+684th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Theon's advantage is a European, sovereign, supply-secured cost-and-access moat in man-portable night vision.

  • It is strong and widening in customer lock-in, regulation and tube priority. It is not a technology or pricing moat.
  • It explains the share and the margin level, not margin expansion. The cost advantage is shared with customers, and the scarce technology and its profit pool sit upstream at Exosens.
  • For the model:
  • The advantage supports stable European NVG share and a stable mid-20s core margin through 2029.
  • Margin expansion must come from mix: non-NVG products without a tube, HGH, and Harder.
  • The new segments carry no moat yet and should be modelled at their own lower margins.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A763rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Moats: which sustainable relative advantages does the company hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: a real, multi-layered moat in European man-portable night vision. It rests on customer lock-in, regulation and priority access to the scarce input more than on technology. It is widening upstream and in regulation, and narrowing slightly on cost as production localises.

  • Customer-related: strong, stable to widening.
  • Military qualification cycles of 2 to 8 years.
  • About 300,000 systems in service, which brings training, spares and upgrades.
  • Custom specifications.
  • Customer relationships since 1997 (Sweden).
  • Each framework award widens the installed base.
  • Production-related: strong, slowly narrowing.
  • Greek assembly gives a structurally low cost base, so prices sit 15% to 20% below Western peers at a 26% EBIT margin.
  • The model is asset-light, with capex about 4% of revenue.
  • The advantage erodes as work moves to Belgium, Germany, France and Middle-East joint ventures under local-content rules, and as Greek wages rise.
  • Scale and access: strong, widening.
  • Theon takes about 52% of Exosens' tube volume to 2030 with priority, which smaller rivals cannot match during an 18-month shortage.
  • The Harder stake is 65.22%.
  • Regulatory and political: strong, widening.
  • ITAR-free and EU-designed.
  • SAFE (65% EU content) and EDIP rules favour it.
  • Culture and knowledge: moderate.
  • Speed and a 20-year team.
  • Technology is not the moat: R&D is 2.8% of revenue, and share gains are "propelled by aggressive sales execution rather than superior technological differentiation".
  • In the new platform, ISR and gimbal segments, Theon has no moat yet; it is buying one through HGH's software and Merio.

C5810th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

  • What the niche scale confers:
  • Exosens' largest-customer status, with Theon at 40% of its revenue.

  • R&D reach: about 12 EURm a year against Thales' billions.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Customer utility: how high is the company's surplus utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

Judgement: non-inferiority is proved. In a shortage market Theon's surplus utility leads on price and availability, though not on top-end performance.

  • Proof: wins of the largest NATO NVG award (OCCAR), the Swedish, German (IdZ, clip-ons), French (ARGUS) and Belgian (IRCOD) programmes, and the Rheinmetall PHYLAX selection.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D3217th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Pricing power: how far does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of customer utility?

Judgement: pricing power is not demonstrated in application. The firm chooses share over price, and the largest contracts are priced by the buyer.

  • Evidence:
  • Industry prices rose with tube costs and were passed through back-to-back.
  • OCCAR is "priced competitively".
  • The gross margin fell from 40.7% (FY2021) to 31.0% (FY2024) on volume discounts, then recovered to 35.1% (H1 2026) on mix.
  • Latent power exists: scarcity, lock-in, and a 15% to 20% price gap to peers that could be narrowed. It is not used, and management does not talk about it.
  • For the model: constant NVG gross-margin percentage plus pass-through of tube cost. No real price uplift.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B607th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Gravitational forces: how far do mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's advantage and its financial outputs each period?

Judgement: the competitive position improves mechanically; the financial outputs do not.

  • Mechanical drivers of share:
  • Each award widens the installed base and lengthens qualification lock-in.
  • Agency consolidation concentrates awards on the largest supplier.
  • Priority tube volume grows with share.
  • Why the financials do not compound mechanically:
  • The model is labour-variable, so each unit is assembled.
  • Headcount went from 135 (2020) to 297 (2023) to 934 (late 2025).
  • Net working capital scales with revenue, from 116 EURm (Q1 2024) to 211 EURm (Q2 2026).
  • New plants and acquired R&D add fixed cost.
  • Consequence: margin is stable, not rising, and cash lags.

Judgement: economically aligned and governance-light. This is a controlled company in which minorities ride alongside the founder's economic interest but have no influence over his capital allocation. The evidence on putting minorities first is mixed to negative.

  • The founder takes no salary, and his wealth is in dividends and equity.
  • He subscribed 107 EURm of the rights issue.

  • A CySEC exemption allowing passive creeping control.

Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

On the operating lines Theon is economically strong: revenue growth of 25% to 35%, a mid-20s EBIT margin at double the defence median, a low tax rate, and an asset-light model with a historic ROCE of 31%.

On the lines that pay the shareholder it is weak:

  • Free cash flow was 17% of net income in FY2025.
  • Working capital is 41% of revenue.
  • Interest and share issuance take about a third of EBITDA growth.
  • Returns on the new acquired capital are unproven.
  • Group ROCE roughly halves through 2027 by construction.

The shape for Set 2:

  • Revenue: NVG at tube-volume-plus-price, and non-NVG roughly doubling over FY2026–FY2028 (my reading of what consensus requires).
  • Margin: EBIT stable at 25.5% to 27%, with OCCAR dilution set against non-NVG and HGH accretion.
  • Below EBIT: interest of about 20 to 25 EURm in FY2027 falling after, tax about 24%, minorities rising to 3 to 5 EURm, about 78.6m shares.
  • Cash: free cash flow is decided by working capital.
  • Operating leverage is low, so the outcome cone is set by financial leverage and mix, not volume.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

A801st of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading EPS growth: what EPS growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement: revenue-led EPS growth of about 20% a year over FY2025–FY2028 on revenue growth of about 25% a year, durable to 2028 on the backlog. Two artefacts in the consensus profile must be removed before modelling, and the EBITDA-to-EPS leak reverses into a tailwind from 2028.

  • History:
  • Brokers'-basis EPS: 0.577 (FY2023), 0.932 (FY2024), 1.16 EUR (FY2025, +24%), on revenue growth of +53%, +61% and +26%.
  • Clean H1 2026 EPS was roughly flat. Clean net income was about 37 EURm against 33.7 EURm, but on 12% more shares. Reported EPS of 0.96 EUR includes 0.47 EUR of Exosens fair-value gains.
  • Consensus, FactSet median:
  • EPS 1.50 / 1.66 / 1.99 / 2.05 EUR for FY2026–FY2029.
  • Revenue +35% / +24% / +17% / +14%.
  • EBITDA +46% / +27% / +19% / +6%.
  • The two artefacts: 1. FY2026 mixes brokers who include the Exosens marks with brokers who exclude them, so the clean base is about 1.26 to 1.30 EUR and the clean FY2027 growth is about +28% to +30%, not +10.7%. 2. FY2029 rests on three brokers and implies about 89m shares.
  • Durability:
  • Contracted to 2028.
  • Interest (9.5 EURm in FY2025, rising to about 20 to 25 EURm) and the 12% share-count rise cost about 4 points a year each, the dilution mainly in 2026.
  • From FY2028, falling net debt adds to EPS growth. This is the leverage-to-EPS transmission running in reverse.

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F1828th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Cash conversion: how high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: poor and the weakest economic line. Working capital is structural rather than cyclical, because it is partly the price of tube priority. Consensus already assumes improvement.

  • FactSet free cash flow over net income:
  • 58% (FY2023), -63% (FY2024), 17% (FY2025).
  • Operating cash flow was 38.0, -23.2 and 48.8 EURm.

  • 41% to 42% of last-twelve-month revenue at Q2 2026 (211 EURm), against a 35% target, Renk's 25%, and Hensoldt's prepayment-funded model.

  • 39 / 85 / 115 EURm for FY2026–FY2028, which is 0.3x, 0.6x and 0.7x net income.
  • My arithmetic: at a constant 41%, the roughly 143 EURm of FY2027 revenue growth would absorb about 59 EURm of working capital. The consensus 85 EURm therefore implies incremental working capital nearer 20% of new revenue, so a falling ratio.
  • Each point of NWC/revenue at FY2027 revenue is about 7.4 EURm of cash.
  • For the model: NWC intensity is the largest free-cash-flow and deleveraging lever. Run 41% (flat), 38% and 35% cases.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F2829th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Financial leverage: how material is it, how much does it amplify EPS and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: material and uncomfortable at the peak in early 2027, comfortable on interest cover. It is the main amplifier of EPS outcomes in the 18-month window, and it caps the multiple until it falls below 2.5x.

  • Net debt 237.9 EURm (1.7x) at H1 2026.
  • Pro forma about 550 EURm in Q1 2027 (my arithmetic: year-end 2026 net debt plus about 290 EURm for HGH), 3.0x to 3.1x LTM EBITDA.

  • Facilities: a 325 EURm five-year term loan to 2031 on better terms than the revolving facility (Euribor plus 1.5%, maturing 2030).
  • Amplification: at 3x leverage a 10% EBITDA miss is about a 13% to 14% EPS miss, and it delays deleveraging by about a year.
  • Mitigant: the Exosens stake (about 306 EURm) could take leverage below 2.0x.

Pricing power

3 answers

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

B486th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute about 1 to 1.5 points a year to group revenue growth over FY2027 to FY2030 and roughly nothing to the margin. Theon is a cost-led price-setter: it passes tube costs through and wins on price, rather than holding real pricing power.

  • The largest single revenue line in the forecast, OCCAR, carries fixed prices. Its tube cost appears locked back-to-back, so it is neither a price contributor nor, on tubes, a squeeze. Its margin risk is wage inflation on the non-tube 45% of cost.
  • Real pricing power exists only in latent form: scarcity, qualification lock-in, a 15% to 20% discount to Western-European peers that could be narrowed, and fused goggles where the discount is "much thinner". It is unlikely to be used before the OCCAR programme ends in 2029.

1. Price by revenue line (my estimates of the shares; none is disclosed).

Line Share of FY2027–FY2029 revenue Price behaviour Contribution to revenue growth Contribution to margin
OCCAR (100,000 binoculars, about 1 EURbn, deliveries 2027–2029) about 35% to 40% of group, 55% to 60% of night vision fixed price; options "at the same or slightly higher prices" (former regional manager, 4 March 2026); no dynamic indexation about 0 about -0.2 points a year on the group, from wage inflation on the non-tube cost
Other night vision (bilateral national, export) about 25% of group follows tube cost: Exosens' average price 2,531 EUR (2025), 2,703 (2026), 2,764 (2027), 2,829 (2028), 2,898 EUR (2029) (Bernstein); passed through back-to-back at a constant gross-margin percentage about +3% to +4% on this line, about +1 point of group about 0
Thermal and digital (IRIS-C, ORION, ARMED) about 10% to 15% no tube, over 40% gross margin (Deutsche Bank); priced as new product mix, not price positive through mix
Platforms, Kappa, PHYLAX about 8% to 10% priced to enter against primes (Deutsche Bank: aggressive); Kappa about 20% EBIT about 0 negative through mix
HGH (counter-drone and search-and-track, with software) about 6% to 7% premium, software-led; over 40% EBITDA +2% to +3% a year positive through mix

2. The insight on OCCAR: the tube risk is hedged, so the margin risk is wages.

  • Exosens booked a 500 EURm order for about 200,000 tubes for the OCCAR programme, delivered 2027 to 2029 (Bernstein, 10 December 2025). That is about 2,500 EUR a tube: the 2025 average price, below the 2,703 EUR Bernstein models for 2026 and the 2,898 EUR for 2029.
  • If, as the order structure implies, this tube price is fixed for the programme, the feared squeeze (fixed end-price, rising tube cost) does not apply to OCCAR's tubes. OCCAR may then earn a better tube margin than the rest of the night-vision book by 2028 to 2029.
  • What remains exposed is the roughly 45% of OCCAR cost that is labour, optics and electronics:
  • the Hensoldt–Theon joint venture is tripling its German staff;
  • Greek wages are rising;
  • inflation of 3% to 5% a year on that share is about 1.5 to 2 points of OCCAR cost a year, unless batch efficiency offsets it.
  • This is why I put night-vision margin down about 0.6 to 0.8 points in FY2027 (OCCAR's competitive price and mix) and then only about 0.2 points a year after.
  • The evidence is thin. The OCCAR escalation terms are confidential. The former head of procurement spoke of annual adjustments capped at about 10%; the former regional manager spoke of no dynamic indexation. Exosens' order terms are not public. This is a research gap (see requests).

3. Why it is pass-through, not power.

  • The 2.5x to 3.0x rise in device prices since 2022 (former regional manager, 1 April 2026) did not lift Theon's margin. Gross margin fell from 40.7% (FY2021) to 31.0% (FY2024). The price rise was tube cost and scarcity rent captured upstream.
  • Management talks about cost advantage, not price. The CFO acknowledged that large contracts need steeper discounts.
  • Peers with real price protection have it in the contract:
  • Elbit's newer contracts carry indexed pricing (Jefferies, 12 August 2026);
  • MilDef's frames protect its gross margin from component prices (DNB Carnegie, 23 October 2026);
  • Hensoldt recognises variable price components only at 80% or higher probability.
  • Theon's frames, on the experts' account, have nothing comparable.

4. Conditions that would change the contribution.

  • Agency buying rising to about 40% of European procurement by 2030 (from about 20%; Deutsche Bank): it shifts more volume to fixed, competitively priced frames. This is already in my mix.
  • A ceasefire or slower procurement after 2027: new frameworks would be awarded into excess capacity. The former regional manager warns of "aggressive price wars". That is a risk to FY2029 to FY2030 pricing; my FY2030 margin of 26.2% carries only about -0.3 points for it.
  • Gulf and export offset rules (Saudi Arabia: 50% local content by 2030; Al Meezan 30% for 12 USDm, 8 April 2026): export prices hold, but local cost rises. Combined with Europe's share of revenue rising (80% of H1 2026), the geographic mix costs about 0.3 points of gross margin over FY2026 to FY2027.
  • Fused and digital goggles: the discount to peers is "much thinner" there (1 April 2026). This is the one route to real price, from 2028, and it is small in the forecast.

5. For the model:

  • Night-vision price growth: about +1.5% a year blended (0% on OCCAR, +3% to +4% on the rest).
  • Non-night-vision: no price uplift; the effect is mix only.
  • No real margin from price.
  • The downside price risk sits in 2029 to 2030 (post-OCCAR re-tendering and a possible ceasefire). The upside sits in fused goggles and HGH software.
5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7555P-0155
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4520th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Operating leverage: how material is the fixed-cost operating leverage, how much does a revenue change move operating profit, and how wide a range does that create?

Judgement: low. Theon's cost base is mostly variable (tubes, components, assembly labour), so a revenue change moves EBIT roughly one-for-one to 1.3-for-one. That is a narrow cone of outcomes from volume alone, which helps predictability and limits upside.

  • Cost structure, FactSet FY2025:
  • Gross margin 32.6%, EBIT margin 23.4%.
  • Opex (SG&A plus expensed R&D) is about 9% of revenue, about 40 EURm, the main fixed block.
  • Within COGS, tubes are 60% to 65% and labour is variable.
  • Sensitivities:
  • My estimate: a 10% revenue shortfall cuts EBIT by about 10% to 13%.
  • Broker sensitivities imply EPS moves about 0.85x to 0.9x the revenue change.
  • Margins held at 25% in the seasonally weak Q1 2026, which shows resilience.
  • Rising fixed cost: new plants (Athens, Belgium), acquired R&D teams (67 Kappa engineers; HGH; Merio) and corporate overhead raise fixed costs from 2027, so operating leverage rises modestly.
  • The real amplifier of outcomes is financial leverage and mix, not operating leverage.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D4520th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage is low, rising slowly, and asymmetric. It will not move the margin much in either direction over FY2026 to FY2030.

  • I hold the adjusted EBIT margin flat at 26.2% to 26.5%, against 26.2% in FY2025.
  • The forces that decide the margin are mix (OCCAR against HGH, thermal and digital) and the step-up in the fixed cost base (localisation, plants, acquired engineering teams). Volume is not one of them.
  • The asymmetry is the important finding for Set 3. Tube supply caps the volume upside that would leverage the fixed block, while a timing slip (option call-offs, Bundestag approvals, a ceasefire) hits revenue with the fixed block already committed.

1. The cost structure: mostly variable, with a fixed block that is growing.

  • Variable: tubes are 50% to 65% of night-vision cost (prospectus, 29 January 2024; Deutsche Bank, 1 October 2025; former head of procurement, 11 March 2026). Assembly labour flexes with contract flow. FY2025 gross margin was 32.6% (FactSet).
  • Fixed: SG&A, expensed R&D (2.8% of revenue) and fixed manufacturing overhead, about 9% to 11% of revenue by my estimate. The block is stepping up:
  • headcount 675 (June 2025) to 1,044 (June 2026), and wages and salaries +10.87 EURm in H1 2026;
  • the Athens plant (about 10 EURm, operational Q2 2027) and the Zaventem plant in Belgium;
  • the Hensoldt–Theon joint venture tripling its German workforce;
  • acquired R&D teams (67 Kappa engineers, HGH, Merio).
  • The quarterly pattern proves there is fixed-cost absorption within the year. The FactSet Q3 margin was 19.1% (2024) and 19.6% (2025) against 24.3% and 23.4% for the full year, and Q4 is the peak. With about 30% to 35% of annual sales in November and December, the fixed block is absorbed in Q4. That makes Q4 delivery timing, not annual volume, the margin swing.
  • The contrast that matters is Exosens, upstream: 60% of its cost is fixed and its gross margin is about 50% (Deutsche Bank). The scarce-component maker owns the operating leverage; Theon, the assembler, does not.

2. The last four to six years: margin fell while revenue rose 5.5 times, because volume was bought with price.

  • FactSet EBIT margin: 31.6% (FY2021), 27.4%, 25.3%, 24.3%, 23.4% (FY2025). Gross margin: 40.7% (FY2021) to 31.0% (FY2024).
  • Deutsche Bank (1 October 2025) attributes the fall to prioritising delivery over price and to contract-size discounts, not to fixed costs.
  • The lesson is that this business has "anti-leverage" on its largest contracts: each step up in contract size has come with a lower unit price. The next four years repeat that through OCCAR, offset this time by mix.

3. The next four years: the margin build by line (my assumptions; no segment margins are disclosed).

EURm, adjusted EBIT margin FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Night vision revenue (margin) 475 (26.6%) 527 (26.0%) 562 (25.8%) 588 (25.8%) 605 (25.5%)
Non-night-vision organic, i.e. thermal, digital, Kappa/platforms, Harder third-party (margin) 127 (26%) 160 (26.5%) 200 (27%) 240 (27.5%) 280 (27.5%)
