I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

A623rd of 30

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: the operating guidance is more likely to be raised than cut at the events inside 18 months (about 60% that it is raised at least once, about 20% that it is cut at least once, and the two can both happen), because the pattern "guide from backlog, raise mid-year, land at the top" has held for three years and the FY2027 backlog is scheduled. The medium-term targets are a different matter: the margin target of above 26% is at risk of an optical miss from the amortisation inside adjusted EBIT, the organic-growth target of above 15% meets the tube cap from FY2028, and the 1 EURbn-by-2029 ambition is about a year early on my arithmetic. The company guides on nothing below EBIT, so the lines where my assessment is below consensus are lines on which guidance cannot be cut.

Event by event (probabilities are my judgement):

Event Date What guidance is in play Raised Met or held Reduced What drives each
Q3 2026 trading update 9 November 2026 FY2026: about 600 EURm, margin above 26% 15% (a tightening to "at least 600" or "about 610" if the Q4 schedule is firm) 80% 5% raised in September already; the nine-month revenue (about 381 EURm) and the order book decide; a cut needs a named Q4 slip
FY2026 results and first FY2027 guidance 19 March 2027 FY2026 delivery; FY2027 revenue, margin, acquired contribution, leverage FY2026 lands at or above 600 EURm: 85%; margin at or above 26% on the company's basis: 80% FY2026 revenue below 598 EURm: 10%; margin below 26%: 20% FY2027 guidance is likely "organic growth above 15%" (about 695 EURm on 605) plus 55 to 60 EURm acquired, so about 750 to 760 EURm, and "above 26%" if the company redefines adjusted EBIT to exclude purchase-price amortisation, or a softer wording if it does not; leverage "below 2.5x by end-2027". Q3 2027 trading update November 2027 FY2027 15% 75% 10% a tightening toward the top of the range
FY2027 results and first FY2028 guidance March 2028 FY2027 delivery; FY2028 FY2027 met or beaten: 75% FY2027 missed: 25% FY2028 guidance is where the tube cap meets the "above 15% organic" framework: my organic growth is about 13%, so the first guidance below the medium-term framework is more likely here than at any earlier event

Why the balance favours raises on revenue and holds on margin:

  • Revenue guidance is built from scheduled backlog (about 500 EURm of FY2027 already scheduled at September 2026, with 902 EURm of options "expected to be exercised shortly"), and the company has beaten the top of its initial range in each of FY2024 and FY2025 and raised FY2026 twice. The first guidance for a year is set with the options outside it; the mid-year raise is the options coming in. That mechanism is intact for FY2027.
  • Margin guidance has been raised once (to above 26%) and delivered at 26.2% for three periods; the H2 2026 and FY2027 mix (HGH, thermal clip-ons) supports it on the brokers' basis. But FY2027 is OCCAR's first bulk year, the Athens and Zaventem plants add fixed cost, and the amortisation question means the company may choose to guide "around 26%" or to restate the basis rather than risk a headline miss. I put the probability of a margin raise inside 18 months at 20% and of a wording change that reads as a reduction at 25%, with the business itself unchanged in most of those cases.
  • A cut to revenue guidance inside the year needs a timing event the company cannot absorb: the base rate for a quarter-plus slip on a German programme (one in three) is the right prior, but Theon's record of pulling other deliveries forward (the 2024 OCCAR option exercised "much earlier than expected"; the 902 EURm of options) and its 18-month lead time on new bilateral orders mean a slip is more likely to show as a smaller beat than as a cut. Hence 15% at the mid-year event rather than 33%.

The medium-term targets, 18 months to three years out:

What our assessment says about the gap to guidance: on the lines the company guides (revenue, margin, capex, mix, leverage) my base is at or slightly above guidance for FY2026 and FY2027 and below the medium-term ambitions from FY2028; on the lines it does not guide (interest, tax, free cash flow, earnings per share) my base is below consensus. Guidance is therefore conservative where I can test it and silent where I disagree, which is favourable for the next two events and neutral after that. The one unfavourable feature is that the market's decisive line (leverage below 2.5x) is the one guided target the record says to lean against, and the one where "met" is a coin toss without the stake.

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C-3813th of 30

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the pattern of the last twelve months (EBITDA up 16%, sales up 11%, EPS down 3%, targets flat) is the likely pattern of the next twelve: operating lines held to nudged up, earnings per share cut by small amounts for accounting and financing reasons, and the breadth of revisions reading negative on EPS and positive on EBITDA. The first cut is nearly mechanical and comes within one to three months: the brokers who include the Exosens marks must take the H2 2026 reversal. The FY2028 and FY2029 consensus is more likely to be cut than raised, on revenue (the tube cap is not in most brokers' numbers) and on interest. The one event that would turn EPS revisions positive is a stake sale or a leverage print well below 2.5x; the sector multiple keeps target prices flat whatever the estimates do.

Where consensus sits against my base (FactSet median, 28 October 2026; my figures after this set's changes):

  • The FY2026 EPS gap is mostly basis (the marks in ING's 1.79 and Marex's 1.95 EUR) plus my tax rate; the FY2027 gap is interest and tax; the FY2028 gap adds the tube cap on revenue; the FY2029 "beat" is the three-broker artefact. My EBIT is at consensus in FY2027 and 3% below in FY2028.
  • The dispersion inside consensus is wider than the gap to it: FY2027 EPS runs from 1.42 (UBS) to 1.91 EUR (Oddo); FY2027 revenue from 712 (Oddo) to 780 EURm (UBS). UBS has the highest revenue and the lowest EPS, which says UBS carries interest and amortisation; Oddo the reverse. The median hides two models of the company.

The likely path of revisions, event by event:

  1. Now to the Q3 update (9 November 2026): FY2026 EPS cut for an accounting reason, operating lines held. Exosens at 56.9 EUR against 61.4 EUR at June (305.9 EURm for 4.98m shares) implies an H2 fair-value loss of about 23 EURm, untaxed. ING's and Marex's FY2026 EPS fall by about 0.30 EUR each when they update; the FactSet mean of the six visible brokers falls from 1.56 to about 1.47 EUR (-6%) and the median from 1.50 to about 1.48 EUR with no operating change. The dashboard's sell-side momentum (C now) would read this as deterioration; it is not. FY2027 EPS revisions stay at -1% to -3% as the brokers who have not yet taken the term loan's interest do so. EBITDA: held to +1% to +2% on the Q3 order and mix detail. Probability of a net negative EPS revision count over the next three months: 70%.
  2. FY2026 results (19 March 2027): operating lines up a little, EPS flat to down, the bases start to converge. The company will likely guide FY2027 revenue at or slightly above the 743 EURm consensus (I-01), and the record argues for brokers moving revenue and EBITDA up 1% to 3%. Against that, the annual report discloses the H2 finance cost, the tax rate by jurisdiction and the Merio purchase-price allocation; if the underlying tax rate prints near 25% or above, and if the company keeps amortisation inside adjusted EBIT, FY2027 EPS comes down 2% to 5% across the brokers who had not modelled either. Net: EBITDA raised (55%), held (35%), cut (10%); EPS cut by 1% to 5% (50%), held (30%), raised (20%).
  3. Q1 2027 and the HGH close (May 2027): consolidation mechanics. Brokers who assumed HGH from January move it to April; FY2027 revenue -5 to -10 EURm, EPS about flat net of interest. A small negative headline revision with no information in it.
  4. H1 2027 results (September 2027): the decisive event for the direction of EPS revisions. A mid-year revenue raise (50%) lifts EBITDA 2% to 4%. If the June leverage prints at or below 2.9x and the company reaffirms below 2.5x by year-end, interest assumptions stop rising and EPS revisions turn flat to slightly positive (40%); if net debt prints worse than consensus for a third time, EPS is cut again for interest and the first Hold rating in a coverage that is 100% Buy becomes likely (25%); if the Exosens stake has been sold, EPS is raised 4% to 5% for FY2028 and the revision count turns decisively positive (the 30% branch).
  5. FY2027 results (March 2028): FY2028 and FY2029 come down on revenue. The FY2028 consensus of 870 EURm (UBS 916, Deutsche Bank 923) implies night-vision growth that the tube supply does not allow without an allocation increase from Exosens; as FY2028 becomes the guided year, I expect revenue cut 2% to 5% and EBITDA 3% to 5% (60%), partly offset by HGH's full year. FY2029 (three brokers, 988 EURm, 2.05 EUR on 89m shares) is cut as more brokers add the year on consistent bases (70%).

The net breadth over 18 months: EPS revisions net negative in most months (the accounting cut, then interest and tax, then the tube cap on FY2028), interrupted by a positive run only if the balance-sheet event arrives; EBITDA and sales revisions net positive but small; target prices flat to down with the sector multiple (ING already cut to 38 EUR on multiples alone); the Buy share can only fall from 100%, and a single downgrade would be read as a signal in a seven-broker coverage. The revision-momentum score of 6 is more likely to fall to about 0 to 3 than to rise over the next six months.

What would break the pattern upward: an Exosens stake sale (EPS up, leverage solved, the bases simplify); a leverage print well inside 2.5x with working capital at or below 40%; FY2027 guidance above 760 EURm with the adjusted-EBIT basis clarified; or Exosens raising its tube allocation to Theon (the one thing that would lift FY2028 revenue consensus rather than cut it). What would break it downward: a Q4 2026 or Q4 2027 collection slip that prints net debt 10% worse than consensus; an OCCAR quarter slipping; an underlying tax rate near 30% confirmed in the annual report.

How this reads against our assessment: the market's revision pattern already points in the direction of my differences with consensus (EPS down, operating lines up), so the direction is known and the magnitude is not: my FY2027 EPS is 2% below the median, my FY2028 EPS 6% below, my free cash flow 18% to 26% below in every year to FY2028. The free-cash-flow line is where consensus is furthest from my view and where the next two net-debt prints can disappoint, and it is also the line the sell-side revises least (it is not in the FactSet revision counts). The favourable element is small and specific: operating consensus is beatable by 1% to 3% at the next two events, and the FY2026 EPS cut that is coming is an accounting event that an informed investor can see through.

Current multiple

C6116th of 30Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0756
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

B586th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: mostly idiosyncratically. Over the latest six months the equal-weighted peer basket explains 24% of Theon's daily price variance (correlation 0.49, beta 0.80); over the last 24 months the share explained has ranged from 4% to 34% and averaged about one fifth. Three quarters of the price action, and by extension of the multiple, is Theon's own. The multiple is therefore company-driven, and what drives it is the balance sheet: the stock de-rated with the sector to a point and then kept falling on its own after the H1 2026 results and the term loan. For predictability that is double-edged: the multiple will respond to Theon's own prints, which the Consumer can forecast, but a sector recovery will carry it only four-fifths of the way and a sector sell-off will still reach it.

The evidence, with the dates that explain it:

  • The rolling six-month R-squared was 4% to 11% through early 2025 (a newly listed stock, thinly traded, moving on its own news), rose to 30% to 34% in August to September 2025 (the sector's order-driven phase, when every defence name moved on budgets and ceasefire odds), held at 22% to 27% through the first half of 2026, dropped to 15% at the end of September 2026 (the window that contains the H1 results, the term loan and the Exosens reversal, when Theon fell while peers held), and is 24% at the end of October. The beta followed the same arc: 0.5 to 0.6 in early 2025, about 1.0 from August 2025 to January 2026, 0.64 at September 2026, 0.80 now.
  • Which peers it moves with: Exosens (correlation 0.51), Hensoldt (0.51), Kongsberg (0.49) and MilDef (0.45), the defence-electronics core; essentially nothing with Melrose, H&K, Astor, Ovzon or Exail (0.01 to 0.16), which dilute the basket. Against the core four alone the explained share would be somewhat higher, perhaps a third, which is still a minority.
  • Returns: six months, Theon -0.9% against the basket -1.3% (in line); three months, Theon -12.6% against MilDef +12.6%, Hensoldt +7.9%, Exail +0.7%, Exosens flat (idiosyncratic); twelve months, Theon +5.0% against Exail +54%, MilDef +59%, Kongsberg +23%, Exosens +20% and Hensoldt -7%. The twelve-month spread between Theon and its closest operating peers is 15 to 55 points, which no sector factor explains.
  • The multiple: 24-month-forward P/E 15.1x (FactSet, 23 October), the 42nd percentile of five years and the 24th of two; the dashboard's P/E 14.7x at the 39th percentile of ten years. Peers: Exosens 21.2x (56th percentile), Hensoldt 28.3x (89th), Kongsberg 25.1x (91st), MilDef 20.4x (37th), Exail 25.2x (71st). The sector de-rated (land-defence EV/EBIT from 21.6x to 17.2x; the sector -18% since mid-August) and Theon de-rated further than the sector, from a two-year median of 17.0x to 15.1x, while its EBITDA estimates rose 16% in the year.

What this says about what the multiple is pricing: the sector factor explains the fall from about 18x to about 16x (the land-defence normalisation that reached every name); Theon's own factor explains the move from about 16x to 15x and the failure to bounce with electronics peers since July. The first set's reading that the market has marked Theon to the question "can a levered founder-led acquirer convert backlog into cash" is consistent with the numbers: the idiosyncratic share rose precisely when the leverage became visible. Bernstein's peer table (Theon 19.8x calendar 2026 P/E against Exosens 26.0x, Hensoldt 47.3x, Exail 33.3x on its basis) and Deutsche Bank's target multiple of 12.6x to 13.5x EV/EBIT at a 15% discount to Exosens both attribute the discount to leverage and cash conversion, not to the sector.

What it means for predictability and for the fair multiple:

  • A multiple that is three-quarters company-driven is a multiple the Consumer can forecast from the company's own events: the end-2026 net debt, the June 2027 leverage test, an Exosens stake sale. That is favourable for a case whose catalysts are balance-sheet prints, because the re-rating does not need the sector to recover. It is unfavourable in that nothing outside the company will rescue the multiple while the leverage question is open; the three months to October showed electronics peers rising 8% to 13% while Theon fell 13%.
  • With a beta of 0.8 to a basket that fell 30% on ceasefire rumours in May to June 2026, a repeat would still take about a quarter off Theon's price even if its own news were unchanged; the first set's stress band (-34% to -41%) is a sector shock on top of an earnings cut, and both legs are live.
  • The fair multiple should be anchored to Theon's own history, not to peers: the guideline 14.8x in the model (between the Selector's 14.4x "conservative" and 15.7x "prudent") is at the 39th to 42nd percentile of Theon's range, which fits a stock in a stalling-to-turning phase whose multiple is set by its own leverage. The peers' 20x to 28x are not a reference until the balance-sheet question closes; if it closes well (leverage below 2.5x or a stake sale), the two-year median of 17x and the 75th percentile of 17.8x become reachable on Theon's own history, which is the re-rating the first set sized at 15% to 20%.
  • The dashboard's technical momentum F, relative-strength percentile 32 and the price 3.6% below a 200-day average that has risen in eleven of the last twelve months describe a stock that has stopped trending with its sector and is drifting on its own: consistent with the co-movement data, and a reason to expect the multiple to stay where it is until a company print moves it.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the eleven ratings page uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D5016th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Precedents: what do precedents, rules of thumb and evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have outperformed and underperformed, and how far does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: the precedents say that, after an order boom, defence shares are paid for delivery, margin and cash, not orders. A large fall with a full order book is usually the company's own cash, debt or share issuance, and it reverses when the cash arrives. Theon fits the second pattern exactly, so its outperformance requires visible deleveraging, not more orders.

Outperformance patterns:

  1. Scarcity owners capture the profit pool. Exosens earns about 50% gross margin against about 31% to 35% for integrators. Theon is the integrator, which is why it buys upstream through Harder and the Exosens stake.
  2. Guidance beats as capacity opens earlier than modelled ("each new production line adds earnings").
  3. Sustained sandbagging on cash. Hensoldt has beaten free-cash-flow guidance every year since its IPO, helped by German prepayments of about 15% of order value; Rheinmetall negotiates about 30%.

Underperformance patterns:

  1. Leverage and dilution after acquisitions. Examples are Exail's PIK-funded iXblue deal, which ended in a sale, and Theon's own December 2025 rights issue.
  2. Fixed-price contracts in inflation. L3Harris, until activist DE Shaw and the LHX NeXt programme.
  3. Programme cancellation. Hensoldt's F126.
  4. Small, geared names sold first in risk-off.

Where Theon fits:

  • It is in the "fall with a full order book" pattern, which the sector digest calls reversible once cash flow is positive.
  • It is not a scarcity owner.
  • It has no prepayment buffer that is visible in its working capital. This is a gap for research: whether OCCAR carries prepayments.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C5510th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: about 20% to 28% to a fair trough, and about 35% to 40% in a stress case with an earnings cut. Against a roughly 15% to 20% re-rating upside, the skew from today is modestly to the downside until leverage falls.

  • Today: 30.42 EUR, 15.08x 24-month-forward consensus P/E (FactSet, 2026-10-23), implying a 24-month-forward EPS of about 2.0 EUR.
  • That multiple is the 42nd percentile of five years and the 24th of the last two.
  • The FactSet EV is 2,564 EURm, excluding the value of the Exosens stake.
  • Fair trough, about 11x to 12x: the 25th percentile (11.16x) and the selector's "crash" band (11.8x), consistent with the sector's exit multiple of about 13x EV/EBIT. That gives about 22 to 24 EUR, -21% to -28%. The 52-week low is 23.34 EUR.
  • Stress: the 10th percentile, 9.86x, on EPS cut about 10% for interest or dilution, gives about 18 to 20 EUR, -34% to -41%. That matches Deutsche Bank's free-cash-flow-yield value of 18.00 EUR and the 17.40 EUR rights price.
  • Upside reference: a re-rating to 17x to 18x (the 75th percentile and the two-year median) gives about 34 to 36 EUR. Targets are about 40 EUR (mean 39.88).
  • Cushion: the Exosens stake (about 3.9 EUR a share at June marks) is a partial floor if monetised.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

A602nd of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative is shifting from "the largest NATO night-vision award and a contracted backlog" to "can a levered founder-led acquirer convert that backlog into cash and deleverage". The market has already marked the stock to that question.

  • Price 30.42 EUR; 3-month return -12.6%; technical momentum F.
  • 24-month-forward P/E of 15.1x, the 42nd percentile, against peers at 20x to 28x.
  • The fall has been idiosyncratic against electronics peers.

The staircase is rich in operating steps, which the market currently discounts, and has one decisive financial step, the H1 2027 leverage print. An asset sale could pull that step forward.

For later sets: the narrative re-rating case and the de-rating case are both mainly balance-sheet stories. The demand story is priced at a median multiple and is not the debate.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–78

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly congruent on the shape of the case, and the few places where they jar are exactly where both sets locate the risk: the lines between EBIT and earnings per share, and the balance-sheet definitions. That is a good sign for predictability, because the disagreements are about the size of known leaks, not about the direction of the business.

The one story both sets tell, in five propositions that every answer supports:

  1. Demand is contracted and policy-backed to 2029 (C-01, C-08, C-32, C-37), so revenue is supply- and schedule-paced, not demand-paced (C-05, P-05, P-10). The second set made the first set's tube ceiling binding, and the OWN-1 report's arithmetic (about 90,000 tubes a year by 2027, of which OCCAR takes about 67,000 in its peak year) agrees with both.
  2. The margin is a mix contest that nets to flat at about 26.4% (C-45, C-46, C-53, P-01, P-04): no pricing power in use, OCCAR dilution against HGH and tube-free accretion, low and asymmetric operating leverage. The template report D-1 finds the same: Greek cost advantage, localisation drift, platform dilution, HGH accretion.
  3. The shareholder's lines are the weak ones: cash conversion of 17% of net income in FY2025, working capital at 41% to 42% of revenue with no prepayments, interest peaking in FY2027, acquired goodwill halving return on capital (C-52, C-76, P-02, P-03, P-08). The OWN-5 report confirms the three structural causes the sets named: tube stockpiles bought for priority, joint-venture receivables, and customer terms of 0 to 120 days with advances of 1.5 EURm.
  4. Leverage is the hinge of the share price, and the H1 2027 print is the decisive event (Group judgements on Risks and Narrative; P-02; P-10). The co-movement table confirms the first set's reading that the fall since July is Theon's own: 76% of its daily variance is idiosyncratic.
  5. Management is a first-class operator with a short record, weak on capital-allocation discipline and disclosure of the EPS-deciding lines (C-57, C-60, C-64, P-06), so the sets lean operating forecasts up and balance-sheet forecasts worse. The FactSet revision pattern (EBITDA +16%, EPS -3% over twelve months; net debt 18% and 7% to 9% worse than consensus at Q1 and H1 2026) is the market's version of the same lean.

Where the answers jar, and how I resolve each:

  • Guidance scored 80 and 75 for being raised and beatable (C-13, C-70) against the second set's finding that the company's adjusted EBIT includes acquired-intangible amortisation (P-08), now confirmed by the Kappa evidence. Both are true, and the contradiction is optical: on the company's current definition, the "above 26%" medium-term target is likely to appear missed in FY2027 (about 25.1%) while the business delivers 26.5% on the brokers' basis. The Consumer must decide which basis the memo tracks; I use the brokers' basis for the forecasts and treat the headline as a Set 4 communication risk (I-01).
  • The first set's "tax is not a leak" against the H1 2026 arithmetic. Resolved by raising the tax rate (reading notes). The first set was right that interest, minorities and dilution are the large leaks; tax is now a small fourth.
  • The 3.5x covenant against the 3.00:1 in the December 2025 information memorandum. Not resolvable from the files; a research request (OWN-3). The sets' cushion arithmetic (about 15% to 17%) holds only if the September 2026 package reset the covenant.
  • The second set's Exosens dividend against the interim report. Resolved by changing net financial items.
  • The first set's pricing (3% to 5% a year on night vision) against the second set's 1.5%. The second set's refinement stands; the OWN-2 evidence that Exosens' prices are fixed at contract entry with annual adjustments supports pass-through on bilateral work and no real price on OCCAR.
  • The first set's 38% largest customer against the H1 2026 interim's 23%. Seasonality (Q4-weighted German deliveries) and the OCCAR lull before the 2027 ramp explain it; the second set's 38% to 40% for FY2026 to FY2028 remains the right planning figure once OCCAR deliveries start.
  • The first set's related-party receivable from the joint venture (34.95 EURm at end-2025) against the OWN-5 report's 6.55 EURm rising to 22.74 EURm at June 2026. Different lines in the related-party note; both say the joint venture ties up cash and the balance is rising into the OCCAR ramp. No conflict of substance.

What does not jar but is now better evidenced: the first set's finding that a full-order-book fall reverses only when the company's own cash arrives (C-84) and the second set's finding that cash improves mechanically from FY2028 as growth slows (P-05, L5 and L9) are the same mechanism viewed from the market and from the model. The OWN-4 report's one-third base rate of quarter-plus slips in German call-offs gives the second set's 20% bear case a documented floor rather than a guess.

What the congruence means for predictability: the sets agree on the ranking of the lines by predictability (revenue, then EBIT, then EPS, then net debt and free cash flow) and on the three checkpoints (FY2026 book-to-bill and options; year-end 2026 and H1 2027 leverage; the OCCAR gross margin). A Consumer working from them will not be pulled in two directions on the operating case. The residual disagreement is confined to about 0.05 to 0.10 EUR of EPS (tax, interest, dividend) and to one definitional question (the covenant) that decides the size of the tail, not the base case.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C4810th of 30

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the narrative is complete on the big financial dynamic and thin everywhere else. The leverage peak, the interest drag on earnings per share, the price paid for HGH and the Exosens stake as a liquidity buffer are discussed by both houses whose research the template report could read (Deutsche Bank on Theon, Bernstein through Exosens), are in management's own words, and are in the price (14.7x, the 39th percentile, technical F). The tube cap, the working-capital realism, OCCAR's margin and the Exosens coopetition are known to one or two analysts as facts but not applied to the out-year numbers. The amortisation inside adjusted EBIT, the untaxed and reversing Exosens marks, the tax rate, the absence of an Exosens dividend, the covenant level and netting, the related-party loans and the January 2027 board renewal are discussed by no one. Investor commentary is absent from the evidence: the report found no Twitter, forum or fund-letter material, and five of the seven covering houses (ING, UBS, Stifel, Marex, Wood, Oddo) are not on AlphaSense, so "how well known" is measured on two of seven, below the 80% the question asked for.

Coverage check: FactSet shows seven houses, all Buy (ING, UBS, Stifel, Deutsche Bank, Marex, Wood & Company, Oddo BHF). AlphaSense holds research from Deutsche Bank (Sriram Krishnan, the lead) and Bernstein (whose Theon views come inside Exosens notes); the report covered both, which is 100% of what AlphaSense holds and 29% of the covering houses. The first set's files add ING's post-H1 note (Tijs Hollestelle: pro-forma leverage to 3.0x against a 3.5x covenant; diversification "much faster than the sell-side is modelling"; target cut to 38 EUR on multiples). Expert calls (two former Theon regional managers, a former head of procurement, former Photonis executives) stand in for the informed non-broker view.

Dynamic by dynamic (prominence: wide = both houses and management; narrow = one house; none = no document found):

Dynamic Who discusses it, since when, and with what view How well known Our assessment against the narrative
EBITDA rising while EPS falls (interest and dilution) Deutsche Bank, explicitly since 11 September 2026 (EPS -5% on the term loan's interest; the December 2025 rights issue at 17.40 EUR as 5% to 11% dilution); visible to every FactSet user in the revision counts (EBITDA +16%, EPS -3%) wide; in the price agreed, and I add tax and the missing Exosens dividend, which nobody has
The Exosens marks in reported EPS and the brokers' inconsistent treatment Deutsche Bank excludes them; ING and Marex include them (FactSet); no document discusses the inconsistency or the H2 2026 reversal; Deutsche Bank in July 2026 expected a 15 EURm charge and got a 37 EURm gain narrow; the mechanics are known to modellers, the H2 reversal and the zero-tax treatment to no one the coming FY2026 EPS cut for ING and Marex is unarticulated; an informed investor can anticipate it
The leverage peak after HGH and the covenant Deutsche Bank (3.4x, 573 EURm, September 2026), Bernstein (3.7x trailing, 3.0x forward, June 2026), ING (3.0x against 3.5x), management (3.0x pro forma, no compliance difficulty, below 2.5x in 2027) wide; the dominant narrative; in the price agreed on the peak; the 3.00:1 in the December 2025 information memorandum and the non-netting of the stake (annual report, Note 39) are discussed by nobody; Bernstein's 1.5x "underlying" figure is wrong for the covenant
The date at which deleveraging becomes visible management ("below 2.5x in 2027"); Deutsche Bank (2.7x late 2027, 2.0x FY2028); the report calls the exact quarter "a critical blind spot" wide in direction, none on the date my view that the H1 2027 report is the pass-or-fail print, and that FY2028's deleveraging is mechanical (growth slowing releases working capital), is not articulated by anyone; the narrative treats deleveraging as execution, not arithmetic
A sale of the Exosens stake Deutsche Bank (management "could monetise financial assets", September 2026); Bernstein (a liquidity buffer that takes leverage below 2.0x) wide as an option; no one puts a probability or a date on it; nobody notes it is tax-free in Cyprus my 30% and the bimodal cone are not in the narrative

The tube-supply ceiling and OCCAR's share of the contracted tubes Deutsche Bank (night-vision growth capped at about 15%, January 2026); Bernstein (OCCAR takes 43% of Exosens' output 2027 to 2029; Theon's framework 52%; December 2025) narrow as a fact; not applied: FY2028 revenue consensus of 870 EURm (UBS 916, Deutsche Bank 923) does not carry it the cap binding on group growth from FY2028 is my largest unarticulated disagreement with consensus
OCCAR's pricing and margin; agency procurement rising to 40% Deutsche Bank (EBIT -3%, "competitively priced", January 2026; agency share 20% to 40% by 2030, November 2025); experts (fixed price, indexation capped near 10%) narrow agreed; the hedge on OCCAR's tubes through Exosens' order and the exposure on the non-tube 45% are discussed by nobody
The shift to tube-free products management (50% by 2029); Deutsche Bank (platforms note, April 2026, "the next growth engine"); ING ("much faster than modelled") wide; it is management's narrative agreed on direction; my 38% by 2029 against 50% is below the narrative
The price paid for HGH and its accretion Deutsche Bank (about 18x pre-synergy EV/EBITDA, "not cheap", over 7x sales); Bernstein (about 10x post-synergy; "reinforcing a key customer's platform, not creating a new direct rival"); management (mid-single-digit EPS accretive in 2027) wide agreed; the amortisation of about 10 EURm a year that follows is discussed by nobody
Concentration on one German customer the annual report (38% in FY2025) and interim (23% in H1 2026); Deutsche Bank (Germany about 60% of revenue) narrow agreed; the OWN-4 base rate of one-in-three slips is new
The 82.66 EURm of interest-free related-party loans (a KPMG key audit matter) none; the interim report does not verify them none an unarticulated governance item; small in cash terms, large for the multiple if it surfaces badly
The board renewal in January 2027 none; the report found it "unpriced by the sell-side" none a free option on governance; also a free risk
Exosens' move into finished devices (NVLS, BiNOD) Bernstein (coopetition; June 2026), Deutsche Bank (October 2025 initiation) wide among the two; not connected to Theon's post-2028 tube allocation my reading that it caps Theon's allocation growth after OCCAR is not articulated
Acquired-intangible amortisation inside the company's adjusted EBIT, and the optical miss of "above 26%" in 2027 none none the most actionable unarticulated item for the next 18 months: a headline margin "miss" that is not one
The tax rate; the absence of an Exosens dividend none none small (0.02 EUR a point; 3 to 5 EURm a year)

What the market is and is not focused on, from the price and the ratings: the P/E at the 39th percentile of ten years, the three-month fall of 12.6% against electronics peers rising, the 100% Buy share with targets 30% above the price, and the technical F together say that the market has priced the leverage worry, has not lost faith in the operating story (no downgrades, targets held), and is waiting rather than selling. Bernstein's observation that investors "penalise leverage" and Deutsche Bank's that the discount to Exosens reflects "lower cash conversion of EBIT" describe the focus accurately: the narrative is about cash, and the stock trades at a 15% discount to Exosens on that basis. What the market is not focused on is anything inside the income statement below EBIT and anything in the accounts' notes.

For the next 18 months: the dynamics that will move the stock (leverage, the stake, net debt prints) are the best-known, so the information edge there is in timing and probability, not in the fact. The dynamics that are unknown are mostly small negatives (tax, dividend, amortisation presentation, related-party loans) and two positives (the mechanical deleveraging from FY2028, and the tax-free liquidity of the stake). For 18 months to three years: the tube cap on FY2028 and FY2029 revenue is the one large dynamic that is known as a fact and absent from the numbers; it is the unarticulated negative that matters most for the out-year consensus.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written date not recorded, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Theon International (THEON-NL): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

As at 2026-10-31. Price 30.42 EUR (2026-10-30). Written for the Analyst building the operating and financial model.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

The source check the instruction asked for. The latest Earnings Review states that it covers "the H1 2026 interim financial results for the six-month period ended 30 June 2026, officially reported on 7 September 2026". It therefore covers the period that FactSet shows as the latest reported (06/2026), and it can be relied on. The earlier review covers Q1 2026 (reported 5 May 2026, revised 12 May 2026).

The five facts the whole model turns on, as I read the files.

  1. Demand is not the constraint through 2028; tube supply, conversion timing and cash are. The soft backlog is 1.46 EURbn at 30 June 2026 plus 902 EURm of contractual options, 2.4x the FY2026 revenue guide of c. 600 EURm, with about 500 EURm already scheduled for FY2027.
  2. The 1 EURbn OCCAR contract is the single largest revenue line of 2027 to 2029, and its margin test is mostly ahead, not behind. Exosens states that its 500 EURm share is 200,000 tubes delivered over 2027 to 2029. Theon's whole Exosens agreement is "well over 400,000" tubes over 2026 to 2030, about 80,000 a year. On that arithmetic OCCAR takes most of Theon's Exosens tube supply in 2027 to 2029. Deutsche Bank expects only about one third of the order to be delivered by end-2027. The H1 2026 gross margin of 35.1% was earned mostly on earlier tranches and on the non-NVG mix, so it does not prove that the new contract will not dilute.
  3. About a third of EBITDA growth is spent before it reaches earnings per share. Interest and the shares issued for the change absorb it. The funding is the December 2025 rights issue of 8.6m shares at 17.40 EUR and peak pro-forma net debt of about 550 EURm, 3.0x to 3.1x EBITDA, in early 2027 after HGH closes, against a 3.5x covenant. From 2028 the same mechanism runs in reverse: deleveraging becomes an EPS tailwind.
  4. Cash conversion is the weakest line. FactSet free cash flow was 16.9% of net income in FY2025 and negative in FY2024. Net working capital is 41% to 42% of last-twelve-month revenue. Consensus free cash flow of 39, 85 and 115 EURm for FY2026 to FY2028 already assumes that working capital improves.
  5. The stock's fall since July is mostly its own balance sheet, not the sector. Theon fell 13.7% in the three months to 28 October 2026, while MilDef rose 12.6%, Hensoldt 7.9% and Exail 0.7%, and Exosens was flat. The re-rating test is Theon's own deleveraging, read at year-end 2026 and in H1 2027.

Data conflicts the model must resolve explicitly.

  • Net debt at 30 June 2026: 237.9 EURm on the company basis against 213 EURm on FactSet's. Use the company's figure for the covenant path.
  • FY2025 adjusted EBIT: 116.1 EURm (26.2%) on the company basis against 104 EURm (23.4%) on FactSet's. The gap is 12 EURm, of which 7.3 EURm are named non-recurring costs.
  • Founder stake: 59.48% indirect at 30 June 2026 (H1 report), against 74% of shares and 71.1% of votes in older files.
  • Revolving facility: cited as both 300 EURm and 400 EURm.
  • Contractual options: 902 EURm, not the "92 EURm" typo in one review.
  • European man-portable share: the company says over 50%; the experts say about 60%.
  • FY2026 consensus EPS of 1.50 EUR mixes two bases. Some brokers include the Exosens fair-value gain (Marex 1.95 EUR, ING 1.79 EUR) and some exclude it (Deutsche Bank 1.28 EUR, UBS 1.43 EUR). The FY2027 consensus "growth" of 10.7% is therefore an artefact. On a clean basis, FY2026 EPS is about 1.26 to 1.30 EUR and FY2027 growth is about 28% to 30%.
  • FY2029 consensus EPS of 2.05 EUR rests on three brokers. It implies about 89m shares against 78.6m in issue, so it is a thin-coverage artefact.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Theon International Plc (THEON-NL), the Euronext Amsterdam-listed, Greece-based maker of man-portable night-vision and thermal-imaging systems for soldiers (its core, about 93% of FY2025 revenue in night vision), expanding into vehicle and platform optronics (Kappa Optronics, the PHYLAX system for Rheinmetall), gyrostabilised gimbals for drones (Merio, completed October 2026; a 51%-owned joint venture with Safran Electronics & Defense), ISR and counter-drone sensors (HGH Systèmes Infrarouges, acquisition expected to close in early 2027), and image intensifier tubes (a 65.22% stake in Harder Digital), with a 9.8% financial stake in its main tube supplier Exosens.

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Exosens (EXENS-FR): Theon's tube supplier, a process manufacturer with a high fixed-cost base, for the upstream cost structure.
  • Hensoldt (HAG-DE): a German optronics peer whose Optronics margin rose with volume in 2025–2026.
  • MilDef (MILDEF-SE): frame-contract hardware and software, with a documented EBITA drop-through.
  • Exail Technologies (EXA-FR): a vertically integrated photonics and navigation group ramping capacity.
  • Elbit Systems: a night-vision goggle prime with its own tubes, at lower margins.
  • L3Harris: the LHX NeXt cost programme, and the sale of its night-vision business to Elbit.
  • Leonardo DRS: sensing-segment margin expansion on volume.
  • Safran Electronics & Defense and Thales: the large European optronics incumbents.
  • Teledyne FLIR: a thermal-imaging peer localising capacity in Europe.
  • Private, where any data exist: Harder Digital (Theon-controlled tube maker), HGH Systèmes Infrarouges, Merio, NightVision Lasers Spain and Senop.

Inputs:

  • Cost split. What is the split between fixed and variable costs for a night-vision system assembler? Cover the share of cost of goods sold taken by image intensifier tubes (Deutsche Bank, 1 October 2025, cites 60% to 65% of manufacturing cost), other components, assembly labour, and selling, general, administrative and R&D costs. How does Theon's split compare with Exosens, Hensoldt Optronics, Elbit and Safran?
  • Seasonality. How has Theon's adjusted EBIT margin behaved in seasonally weak quarters against Q4? The company reported 25.0% in Q1 2026 and 26.2% in H1 2026. What does that say about its fixed costs?
  • Cycle behaviour. How did integrator and component-maker margins move with volume in past up-cycles and down-cycles of night-vision and optronics procurement, with dated figures? Cover the post-Cold-War and post-2010 budget cuts in Europe and the US, and the 2022–2026 rearmament.
  • New fixed costs. What fixed costs are Theon's new plants (the third Athens facility, about 10 EURm, expected mid-2027; Theon Belgium in Zaventem) and its acquired engineering teams (Kappa added 67 R&D engineers) adding? How have comparable capacity additions affected margins at Hensoldt (Oberkochen), Exosens (Brive, Sturbridge, Mérignac) and MilDef (Helsingborg)?
  • Labour-cost durability. How durable is the Greek labour-cost advantage, given local-content assembly in Germany, Belgium, France and Middle-East joint ventures, and given Greek wage inflation? What have localisation mandates done to margins at comparable defence suppliers?
  • Cost programmes. What cost programmes were announced at comparable optronics and defence-electronics companies, what did they deliver against promise, and how long did they take? Cover L3Harris LHX NeXt, the Hensoldt integration of ESG Group, and Exail's integration of iXblue.
  • Integration savings. What purchasing and R&D savings have followed the integration of acquired optronics businesses? Theon states cost synergies "by strengthening purchasing power with common supplies" for Merio (press release, 4 May 2026) and expects 30 EURm of revenue synergies by 2029.
  • Cuttable lines. Which cost lines proved cuttable in downturns at defence-electronics assemblers (labour, opex, capex, R&D), which did not, and why?

Focus: How much of Theon's cost base is now genuinely fixed, after the new plants, the acquired engineering teams and the integration overhead? How would its operating margin move if deliveries under its largest framework contracts or option call-offs slipped by a year? And have comparable optronics integrations delivered real cost savings?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • Exosens (EXENS-FR): the tube supplier, with a stated record of tube price increases.
  • Hensoldt (HAG-DE): German procurement terms and prepayments.
  • Rheinmetall: framework contracts with large downpayments.
  • Elbit Systems: indexation clauses in newer contracts, and NVG pricing in the US and the UK.
  • L3Harris: fixed-price contracts in the 2022–2023 inflation.
  • Safran Electronics & Defense and Thales: European handheld optronics pricing.
  • MilDef (MILDEF-SE): fixed-price frame contracts and component inflation.
  • Teledyne FLIR: thermal pricing.
  • Private: NightVision Lasers Spain (owned by Exosens), Sunrock (Theon's French law-enforcement channel), JK Defence (German channel).

Operating conditions:

  • Large multinational agency volume tenders, such as OCCAR frameworks, against bilateral national awards.
  • Tube-cost inflation and tube scarcity, with lead times of about 18 months.
  • A ceasefire or slower procurement urgency in Europe.
  • Export markets with offset and local-content requirements (the Middle East, South Korea).
  • Acting as a subcontractor to a prime (Rheinmetall for PHYLAX; Elbit Systems of America for US Marine Corps binoculars) rather than as prime.
  • Multi-year fixed-price frameworks during general inflation and higher interest rates.

Inputs:

  • The OCCAR contract. What are the price, escalation and payment terms of the c. 1 EURbn OCCAR framework for 100,000 night-vision goggles for Germany and 4,000 for Belgium (signed December 2025)? Does it carry indexation, downpayments or milestone payments? How does the unit price compare with national bilateral contracts?
  • Supply-agreement terms. What are the price and indexation terms of the Theon–Exosens long-term supply agreement, extended in December 2025 to end-2030 for "well over 400,000" tubes? A former EOTECH executive, on an expert call of 3 March 2026, described a fixed annual escalation of about 5% as typical. Bernstein, 23 September 2026, models Exosens' amplification selling price rising from 2,531 EUR in 2025 to 2,898 EUR in 2029. Are Theon's end contracts genuinely back-to-back on tube cost?
  • OCCAR tube allocation. Exosens states that its c. 500 EURm share of the OCCAR contract covers 200,000 tubes delivered over 2027 to 2029. How many tubes under the Theon agreement are allocated to that programme, and what remains for Theon's other customers?
  • Price against peers. Is the reported 15% to 20% price discount of Theon's legacy night-vision systems to Western European peers (former regional manager, expert call, 1 April 2026) narrowing on fused and digital goggles?
  • Price history. How have night-vision device prices moved since 2022? An expert call put the rise at 2.5x to 3.0x. How much of that was tube pass-through, and how much was retained by integrators?
  • Margin attribution. Was the rise in Theon's gross margin to 35.1% in H1 2026, from 32.7% in H1 2025, from price, from product mix (non-night-vision products without a tube) or from Harder Digital yields?
  • Precedents in each condition. How did integrator and supplier pricing behave in each named condition in the past, with dated figures? Cover price given back on large tenders, and contract renegotiation after cost shocks at Elbit, L3Harris and MilDef.
  • Contract terms elsewhere. What contract structures, pass-through clauses, indexation and payment lags apply in German (BAAINBw), French and Swedish soldier-optronics frameworks? What prepayments do Hensoldt (about 15% of order value, 2026) and Rheinmetall (about 30% on large frameworks) obtain, and could Theon obtain the same?
  • Customer lock-in. How concentrated are Theon's customers (one European customer was 38% of FY2025 revenue, per the 2025 annual report), and how high are switching costs after qualification?
  • Current commentary. What do customers, competitors, experts and the sell-side say now about pricing in man-portable night vision, thermal clip-ons, vehicle optronics and drone gimbals?

Focus: Can Theon hold its gross margin as deliveries under the competitively priced OCCAR framework rise over 2027–2029? Does it have any contractual protection against tube-price increases on fixed-price frameworks? And does it have any pricing power in its new platform and gimbal segments?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The case contains several self-reinforcing and self-limiting dynamics: tube priority and market share, debt-funded acquisitions and the share price, and the co-dependence with Exosens. They could amplify outcomes in either direction over 2027–2029, so their direction and strength matter more than any single line.

Peers:

  • Exosens (EXENS-FR): the mutual dependency with Theon, and its own downstream move into goggles.
  • Exail Technologies (EXA-FR): a debt- and private-equity-funded acquisition that ended in a takeover by Thales.
  • Hensoldt (HAG-DE): prepayment-funded capacity expansion.
  • Rheinmetall: order-funded expansion and its de-rating.
  • Elbit Systems and L3Harris: vertical integration in tubes, and US market lock-out.
  • MilDef (MILDEF-SE): frame contracts and capacity doubling.
  • Private: Harder Digital.

Inputs:

  • Tube-priority loop. Does buying the largest share of Exosens' tube output secure priority and faster delivery, which wins more framework awards, which justifies more volume? Is this loop operating now, and what would stall it (Exosens capacity, Exosens' own goggle sales through NVLS)?
  • Agency-consolidation loop. Does winning large agency awards (OCCAR) widen the installed base and qualification lock-in and so favour follow-on awards? Or does buyer power rise with each larger award?
  • Supplier-integration loop (balancing). Does Exosens' downstream entry (the NVLS acquisition in July 2025; 17,000 Spanish Army monoculars; a US Army BiNOD award) create an incentive to allocate scarce tubes away from Theon? How does Theon's 9.8% stake and 40% share of Exosens' revenue (Bernstein) restrain this?
  • Leverage-dilution loop. Do debt-funded acquisitions raise interest costs and slow EPS growth, which lowers the multiple, which raises the cost of equity and increases the risk of a discounted equity raise? The December 2025 rights issue was priced at 17.40 EUR. Pro-forma leverage of about 3.0x is expected after the HGH closing (company, September 2026), against a 3.5x bank covenant.
  • Working-capital loop. Do tube stockpiling and fast supplier payment, used to keep priority, absorb cash, slow deleveraging and constrain further investment? Net working capital was about 40% of last-twelve-month revenue in H1 2026 (company).
  • Deleveraging and re-rating loop. Would selling the Exosens or Kopin stakes, or organic cash generation, reduce leverage, lower the discount to peers and lower the cost of capital for further growth? Precedents of the same loop at other defence or industrial mid-caps are needed.
  • Localisation loop (balancing). Do content rules push assembly into higher-wage countries, eroding the cost advantage that won the awards?
  • Precedents. For each loop: how it works step by step, the dated evidence that it is operating now, past cases where the same loop ran (how far, how long, what stopped it), and what would strengthen, stall or reverse it.

Focus: Which of these loops are active now, and in which direction will each push revenue, margin, net debt and the share count over 2027 to 2029? The leverage-dilution loop and the tube-priority loop matter most.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The path from operating profit to earnings per share is where this company's case is decided. Brokers are inconsistent on it: FY2026 EPS estimates range from 1.28 EUR (Deutsche Bank) to 1.95 EUR (Marex) on FactSet, partly on the treatment of fair-value gains on the Exosens stake. The interest line, the covenant and working capital are not guided by the company.

Peers:

  • Exosens (EXENS-FR): leverage, R&D capitalisation and the gap between adjusted and reported earnings.
  • Hensoldt (HAG-DE): prepayments, R&D capitalisation, and covenant and leverage targets.
  • MilDef (MILDEF-SE): acquisition-funded deleveraging and minority structures.
  • Exail Technologies (EXA-FR): acquisition financing costs, purchase-price amortisation and adjusted-earnings practice.
  • Leonardo: its leverage covenant (3.75x, per its annual report) as a comparison for Theon's 3.5x.

Inputs:

  • Debt costs. What are the pricing, tenor, fixed or floating basis and hedging of the 325 EURm five-year syndicated term loan signed 7 September 2026 (coordinated by Alpha Bank)? And of the revolving credit facility (Euribor plus 1.5%, maturing 2030, per the H1 2026 report)? How much is drawn, and what net interest should be expected as the HGH and Merio payments are made?
  • Covenant definition. What is the bank covenant definition of net debt to EBITDA? Are leases included? Can the Exosens stake be netted (Bernstein, 18 June 2026, calculates leverage both ways)? Is acquired EBITDA included pro forma? How much headroom is there at the expected peak after the HGH closing?
  • Acquisition cash. What are the timing and amount of cash paid for HGH (enterprise value about 300 EURm; ING expects about 290 EURm of cash in Q1 2027) and for Merio (closed 29 October 2026)? What revenue, EBITDA and purchase-price amortisation will each add in 2027?
  • Amortisation and adjustments. How will purchase-price amortisation and other adjustments be treated in the company's adjusted EBIT and by each broker? FY2025 company-adjusted EBIT was 116.1 EURm against 104 EURm on FactSet's operating basis.
  • The Exosens stake. How is it accounted for (fair value through profit and loss)? How did each broker treat the 37.2 EURm H1 2026 fair-value gain in FY2026 and FY2027 EPS? Does the stake pay or accrue any income?
  • Minorities. How large will the minority interests become (Harder Digital 34.78%, Merio 20%, the Safran gimbal joint venture 49%, the Twin Prime joint venture 40%)?
  • Tax. What effective tax rate should be expected, including the Greek Development Law 4887/2022 incentive and the tax rates in France and Germany after the acquisitions?
  • Share count. What is the share count path, including the 1.4m-share buyback for the incentive plan? What precedents exist for equity raises by comparable companies when leverage approached covenants?
  • Related-party loans. What are the nature, counterparties, recoverability and cash-flow implications of the 82.66 EURm of interest-free related-party loans receivable flagged by KPMG as a key audit matter (2025 annual report)?
  • Working capital. What drives working capital: receivables through the Hensoldt–Theon joint venture (34.95 EURm at end-2025), inventory and tube stockpiling, supplier payment terms, and the presence or absence of customer prepayments on OCCAR and German frameworks? How have peers reduced working-capital intensity?
  • Capex and dividends. What capex is planned (30 EURm budgeted for FY2026) and what is the dividend policy (payout of 20% to 30% of net profit)?
  • For each input: how it has behaved historically and why, what management guides, what the sell-side assumes and how far apart the brokers are, and what would move it materially either way.

Focus: What does a clean and consistent earnings-per-share bridge for FY2026 to FY2028 look like? What is the path of net debt and covenant headroom through the HGH closing in early 2027? And which of interest, working capital, minorities and the Exosens accounting is most likely to make reported earnings per share differ from the operating trajectory?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 11:28 to 11:51 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Theon International (THEON-NL): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

As at 2026-10-31. Price 30.42 EUR (2026-10-30). Written for the Analyst answering the third and fourth sets.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

The model in one paragraph

Theon's next four years are a supply-capped, mix-driven, leverage-taxed version of its last four. Revenue grows from 443 EURm (FY2025) to about 605 EURm (FY2026), 752 EURm (FY2027), 853 EURm (FY2028), 946 EURm (FY2029) and 1,027 EURm (FY2030): +36%, +24%, +13%, +11%, +9%. The adjusted EBIT margin holds at 26.2% to 26.5% throughout: neither the expansion a fixed-cost business would show nor the collapse that two former employees predict. Adjusted EPS grows from 1.16 EUR (FY2025, brokers' basis) to 1.39 / 1.68 / 1.93 / 2.19 / 2.39 EUR, about 18.5% a year to FY2028 and 11% a year after. Net debt peaks at about 2.9x to 3.0x EBITDA in the first half of 2027, falls to 2.4x at end-2027 and to 2.0x at end-2028. Free cash flow conversion rises from 26% of adjusted net income (FY2026) to about 70% (FY2029–FY2030).

The bases used throughout (read before comparing any figure with consensus or with the model's history rows)

  • Adjusted EBIT is the company's adjusted EBIT: reported EBIT before non-recurring items. It was 116.1 EURm (26.2%) in FY2025 and 65.14 EURm (26.2%) in H1 2026.
  • From 2027 I add back the amortisation of acquired intangibles from HGH and Merio, as brokers normally do.
  • Kappa: the H1 2026 bridge from reported EBIT (61.25 EURm) to adjusted EBIT (65.14 EURm) is exactly the non-recurring items (3.89 EURm). So whatever amortisation Kappa's 27.1 EURm of acquired intangibles generates (my estimate: about 3 EURm a year) already sits inside the company's adjusted EBIT and inside my input. I do not add it back, because the treatment is not confirmed and the effect is about 0.4 points.
  • FactSet's EBIT line is lower: 104 EURm (23.4%) in FY2025. It is after the 7.3 EURm of non-recurring costs and about 5 EURm of other items, most likely the Hensoldt joint-venture income that the company moved into operating profit from FY2025 (Deutsche Bank: worth +80 basis points). The model's history row (23.4%) and my FY2026 input (26.4%) are on different bases. The 3-point jump is a basis change, not a margin step-up.
  • Net financial items are all finance income and costs except the Exosens fair-value marks, which I set at zero in the forecast. They include the Exosens dividend, about 3 EURm a year.
  • Adjusted EPS (my cross-check):
  • It is adjusted EBIT plus net financial items, taxed, less minorities, divided by basic shares net of treasury shares (77.6m to 78.2m). On the 78.6m diluted count it would be about 1% lower.
  • It excludes non-recurring items, HGH and Merio purchase-price amortisation and the Exosens marks.
  • Deutsche Bank's 1.28 EUR for FY2026 appears to sit after non-recurring items. Marex (1.95 EUR) and ING (1.79 EUR) include the Exosens marks.
  • Reported EPS is my adjusted EPS less non-recurring items, less HGH and Merio amortisation, plus the Exosens marks. It is shown in P-08.

The ten conclusions the Consumer most needs

  1. Night-vision revenue is capped by tubes, and the cap binds from 2027. - Supply: Exosens agreed "over 400,000" tubes for 2026 to 2030, about 80,000 to 90,000 a year. Harder adds about 13,000 a year for internal use. - Demand on that supply: the OCCAR programme alone needs about 200,000 tubes over 2027 to 2029, about 67,000 a year (Bernstein, 10 December 2025). - Result: night-vision revenue grows about 11% in FY2027 (475 to 527 EURm), then 4% to 7% a year. Group growth beyond FY2027 depends on non-night-vision lines. - Management's own 2029 plan (1 EURbn of revenue, half of it non-night-vision) implies flat night vision at about 500 EURm. The plan agrees with the cap; it is the non-night-vision half that is ambitious.
  2. Volume upside is capped but volume downside is not, so operating leverage is asymmetric. A 10% revenue shortfall cuts EBIT by 11% to 13% and adjusted EPS by about 15%. A 5% beat lifts EPS by only about 7%, and only non-night-vision lines can deliver it.
  3. The margin path is a contest of mix, not of operating leverage. Against it: OCCAR (competitively priced), wage localisation (headcount 675 to 1,044 in the year to June 2026), Kappa and platforms at about 20%. For it: HGH (over 40% EBITDA), thermal and digital products (over 40% gross margin, with no tube). The net is flat at about 26.4%.
  4. Price is pass-through, not power. The OCCAR tubes appear locked at about 2,500 EUR each through Exosens' 500 EURm OCCAR order for about 200,000 tubes, against Bernstein's 2026 average of 2,703 EUR. This makes OCCAR's tube cost a hedge, not a squeeze. Its margin risk is the non-tube 45% of cost (wages). Price adds about 1 to 1.5 points a year to group revenue and roughly nothing to margin.
  5. The below-EBIT leak peaks in FY2027 and then reverses. - Net financial items move from -6.8 EURm (FY2025, company basis) to -18 EURm (FY2026) and -25 EURm (FY2027), then fall to -16 EURm (FY2030). - Adjusted net income over adjusted EBIT falls from 0.71 (FY2025) to 0.65 (FY2027) and recovers to 0.69 (FY2030). - Deleveraging adds about 1 to 2 points a year to EPS growth over FY2028 to FY2030.
  6. The leverage test is knife-edge. End-2027 net debt over EBITDA is about 2.44x in my base, against the company's target of "below 2.5x in 2027". - Four things push it above 2.5x: one more point of working capital (about 7.5 EURm), 15 EURm less EBITDA, a 2027 bolt-on, or a slip in the Q4 2027 collection timing. - The base case therefore meets the target only narrowly, and the market will read the H1 2027 print (September 2027) as pass or fail.
  7. Reported earnings will diverge from adjusted earnings from 2027. - HGH and Merio amortisation of about 10 to 11 EURm a year costs about 0.10 EUR a share after tax. With the remaining non-recurring costs, my FY2027 reported EPS is about 1.53 EUR against an adjusted 1.68 EUR. - If the company keeps acquired-intangible amortisation inside its own "adjusted EBIT", as the H1 2026 bridge suggests it did for Kappa, its headline adjusted margin falls about 1.3 to 1.4 points in 2027. The ">26%" target would then appear missed while the business is unchanged.
  8. The Exosens mark reverses in H2 2026. At 56.9 EUR (30 October 2026) the 4.98m shares are worth 283 EURm, against 305.9 EURm at 30 June. That is a fair-value loss of about 23 EURm in H2 and a full-year gain of only about 14 EURm (37.2 EURm at H1). Brokers who include the marks (ING 1.79 EUR, Marex 1.95 EUR) must come down, so FY2026 consensus EPS will drift lower for an accounting reason.
  9. Free cash flow improves mainly because growth slows, not because the working-capital ratio improves. - Working-capital absorption falls from about 48 EURm (FY2026) to 27 EURm (FY2029–FY2030), while the ratio moves only from 42.5% to 38.5% of revenue. - My FactSet-definition free cash flow is 32 / 63 / 94 / 120 EURm for FY2026 to FY2029, against consensus of 39 / 85 / 115 / 105 EURm: 19% to 26% below in FY2026 to FY2028 and above in FY2029.
  10. The track record supports leaning revenue and EBIT slightly above consensus for FY2026 and FY2027, and the balance sheet slightly worse. But the record was built in a demand-pull, short-cycle, net-cash phase. The next 18 months are fixed-price, supply-capped, leveraged and multi-country, so the beats should be expected to continue at smaller amplitude.

Where I disagree with the first set, and why

  • Clean FY2026 EPS of 1.26 to 1.30 EUR (first set): I put adjusted EPS at 1.39 EUR, and about 1.31 EUR after non-recurring costs. Two reasons: 1. Basis. Adjusted EBIT excludes the non-recurring costs (about 8.5 EURm in FY2026), so a consistent adjusted EPS must also exclude them. This is worth about 0.08 EUR. 2. H1 2026 finance costs are ambiguous (see the next point). The first set's 1.26 EUR implicitly took the expensive reading.
  • It follows that the clean FY2027 growth is about +21% (1.39 to 1.68 EUR), not the first set's +28% to +30%. The first set was right that consensus's +10.7% is an artefact; it overstated the true figure.
  • The H1 2026 finance-cost and tax split is unresolved, and it moves FY2026 EPS by about ±0.06 EUR. Reported net profit of 74.3 EURm, the 37.2 EURm Exosens gain and the 19.9% interim tax rate fit two readings:
  • (a) The fair-value gain was taxed (deferred tax). Then H1 net finance cost was about 5.7 EURm and clean H1 net income about 43 to 45 EURm.
  • (b) The gain was untaxed and the 19.9% applied to operating profit. Then H1 net finance cost was about 15 EURm, which fits the reported +158% rise in interest expense better, and clean H1 net income was about 37 EURm (the first set's figure).
  • I take the midpoint, -10 EURm for H1. The FY2026 results on 19 March 2027 resolve it; the Q3 trading update will not.
  • The first set's revenue predictability for FY2026 and FY2027 is high: agreed. But I go further on the tube ceiling it identified (500 to 600 EURm of night-vision revenue) and make it binding. That puts my FY2028 revenue 2% below consensus (853 against 870 EURm) and FY2029 4% below (946 against 988 EURm, three brokers), and it misses the 1 EURbn target by a year.
  • Pricing (first set C-17: price is 3% to 5% a year on night vision): refined. The OCCAR share of night vision (about 55% to 60% in 2027 to 2029) carries fixed prices, with tubes locked through the Exosens order. Price therefore contributes about 1.5 points of night-vision growth, not 3 to 5.
  • The first set did not flag two things:
  • that the company's own adjusted EBIT appears to include acquired-intangible amortisation, which threatens the headline ">26%" margin from 2027;
  • the size of the H2 2026 reversal of the Exosens mark.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Night vision is capped by tube supply.
  • Supply: Exosens agreed "over 400,000" tubes for 2026 to 2030 (3 December 2025), about 80,000 to 90,000 a year. Harder Digital adds about 13,000 a year for internal use (15,000 capacity in 2025, 20,000 in 2027, about 66% internal).
  • Demand on that supply: the OCCAR programme (100,000 binoculars, about 1 EURbn, deliveries 2027 to 2029) needs about 200,000 Exosens tubes, about 67,000 a year (Bernstein, 10 December 2025).
  • Night-vision revenue therefore grows at tube volume plus price: +11% in FY2027, then +4% to +7% a year. Price is about +1.5% a year blended: zero on OCCAR's fixed price and +3% to +4% on bilateral and export contracts, passing through Exosens' tube price rises (Bernstein: 2,531 EUR in 2025 to 2,898 EUR in 2029).
  • Options (902 EURm) and pre-selections lengthen the backlog rather than raising annual revenue.
  • Non-night-vision organic growth runs at 26%, 25%, 20% and 17%. The sources:
  • IRIS-C thermal clip-ons (OCCAR IRCOD: 50 EURm initial order plus 150 EURm of options);
  • Kappa (42 EURm SAFE-funded order) and PHYLAX for Rheinmetall (over 40 EURm initial);
  • Harder third-party tubes (a record 25 EURm order for 2026 to 2027);
  • ORION fused and ARMED digital goggles.
  • Acquired revenue in FY2027 is about 60 EURm (management: 55 to 60 EURm of 2027 sales from HGH and Merio). HGH is about 40 EURm of revenue at an EBITDA margin above 40%; Merio is over 15 EURm of revenue and over 3.5 EURm of EBIT in 2026.
  • The margin is held flat by mix.
  • Against it: OCCAR is competitively priced (about -0.6 to -0.8 points on night vision from FY2027; Deutsche Bank cut EBIT 3% on it), platforms come in at about 20%, wages are rising through localisation (headcount 675 to 1,044 in the year to June 2026; the joint venture tripling its German staff; Zaventem; Athens plant from Q2 2027), and Europe's share of revenue is rising.
  • For it: HGH, thermal and digital lines without a tube (over 40% gross margin), and Harder's yields.
  • Operating leverage is low: tubes and assembly labour are variable, and fixed costs are about 9% to 11% of revenue.
  • Net financial items: about 4.5% all-in on average net debt (the 300 EURm revolving facility at Euribor plus 1.5% to 2030; the 325 EURm five-year term loan of 7 September 2026), plus about 4 EURm a year of FX, guarantee and fee costs, less the Exosens dividend.
  • Average net debt is about 245, 540, 525, 470 and 400 EURm for FY2026 to FY2030.
  • FY2026 carries ±5 EURm of uncertainty, because the H1 2026 split between finance costs and tax cannot be pinned down from the published totals: -5.7 EURm or about -15 EURm for H1, depending on whether the Exosens gain was taxed. I take the midpoint.
  • Net debt (company definition): about 250 EURm at end-2026 (1.5x), about 540 EURm pro forma after HGH (about 2.9x), a mid-2027 peak of about 575 EURm (about 2.9x to 3.0x), then about 515 EURm at end-2027 (2.44x), 475 EURm at end-2028 (2.0x), 420 EURm (1.6x) and 350 EURm (1.2x).
  • Minorities (Harder 34.78%, Merio 20%, Safran joint venture 49%, Twin Prime joint venture 40%): 1.5, 2.5, 3.5, 4.5 and 5.5 EURm. The form has no minorities line. My cross-check net income is after minorities, so the model's figure will be higher by these amounts (up to about 0.07 EUR a share).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • EBIT. My input is the company's adjusted EBIT, which is reported EBIT plus non-recurring items (FY2025: 7.3 EURm; H1 2026: 3.89 EURm, giving 61.25 to 65.14 EURm). From FY2027 I add back HGH and Merio acquired-intangible amortisation (about 10.4, 11.2, 11.2 and 10.0 EURm for FY2027 to FY2030).
  • FactSet's EBIT is lower by about 12 EURm in FY2025 (104 against 116.1 EURm): the non-recurring items plus about 5 EURm, most likely the Hensoldt–Theon joint-venture income the company reclassified into operating profit.
  • Consensus EBIT (155 / 199 / 231 / 250 EURm) is on brokers' adjusted bases. FY2026's 25.8% is below the above-26% guidance, which suggests some brokers deduct non-recurring items.
  • EBITDA. Consensus EBITDA is brokers' adjusted (FY2025 base 112 EURm, 25.2%); FactSet's operating EBITDA is 108 EURm. Mine is adjusted EBIT plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles: 167.7, 210.3, 238.2, 265.7 and 286.1 EURm.
  • Consensus D&A jumps from 10.7 EURm (FY2027) to 15.8 EURm (FY2028), which suggests that some brokers deduct HGH amortisation and others do not.
  • EPS. Consensus FY2026 EPS (median 1.50 EUR) mixes brokers who include the Exosens fair-value marks (Marex 1.95 EUR, ING 1.79 EUR) with brokers who exclude them (Deutsche Bank 1.28 EUR, apparently also after non-recurring costs; UBS 1.43 EUR).
  • My adjusted EPS excludes the marks, the non-recurring items and HGH and Merio amortisation: 1.39 EUR.
  • My reported-basis EPS is about 1.46 EUR. It includes a full-year Exosens gain of about 14 EURm pre-tax, because the stake was worth 305.9 EURm at June and about 283 EURm at 56.9 EUR on 30 October 2026, implying an H2 loss of about 23 EURm.
  • Reported EPS for FY2027 to FY2030: about 1.53, 1.80, 2.07 and 2.29 EUR.
  • The consensus FY2029 EPS (2.05 EUR, three brokers) implies about 89m shares against 78.6m. It is an artefact; on net income, consensus is 183 EURm against my 171 EURm.
  • Net debt. The company figure was 237.9 EURm at 30 June 2026; FactSet's was 213 EURm (debt 334 including leases, cash 122). Use the company basis for the covenant.
  • Shares. I use basic shares net of treasury (77.6m to 78.2m average). FactSet's market capitalisation uses 78.6m diluted, which would lower EPS by about 1%.
